The Leaders AI Innovation Needs
2026-09-08

The Leaders AI Innovation Needs

TIME | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings For over 25 years, I have studied exceptional leaders of innovation, individuals from a range of companies and countries, from the Cleveland Clinic Abu Dhabi to Pixar. For hundreds of hours, I have observed leaders who have figured out how to get their colleagues to adopt emerging technologies to innovate and change the game for their customers. What I have learned from doing this work is this: leading AI innovation is not about having a vision and getting others to follow you to the future; rather, it is about inviting others to co-create the future with you. This is also true of the AI age. While virtually every company is scrambling to adopt new AI practices, the leaders who have made the most progress are those who believe the value of AI will come from using the technology to do work people could never do themselves, and the least value will come from simply swapping machines for people. Their goal is to pursue solutions that augment human ingenuity. And they appreciate that their toughest challenge is about people, not technology. These leaders know that we all need help navigating uncertainty, and they encourage others to try out new ways of working despite real intellectual and emotional demands. Innovation is rarely the feat of a solo genius but rather the fruit of co-creation, which requires collaboration, experimentation, and learning from people with differing expertise and perspectives. Collaboration entails weaving together diversity of thought through constructive conflict to create a marketplace of ideas. Experimentation involves actively testing, pivoting, and refining. And learning happens when individuals and collectives reflect on what they have gleaned from those experiments—and then adapt their course of action. To generate and scale innovative AI solutions, leaders rely on a repertoire of interrelated roles, what we refer to as the “ABCs” of leadership: architects, bridgers, and catalysts. Architects know they cannot mandate innovation; they foster the culture and capabilities that enable co-creation. These leaders start by raising their organization’s collective aspirations through a shared sense of purpose and values. By re-shaping the social environment of their organizations, these leaders encourage their colleagues to work through the inevitable conflicts of collaborating with others and the fear of failure. They reward thoughtful risk-taking, treat intelligent failures as learning opportunities, and provide people the tools, data, and perhaps most importantly, the permission to try. Bridgers work at the boundaries of their enterprises, building trust-based partnerships with those outside their walls. No company, no matter how well-resourced, has all the talent and tools they need for innovation given the unprecedented pace at which technology is advancing. With AI, I hear that there is a shortage of individuals who know how to translate and work across technology and business. Technical experts who are developing AI solutions typically do not have the contextual intelligence about the realities of business and what customers want, while the businesspeople do not appreciate the opportunities and risks of implementing the technology. Catalysts have to mobilize stakeholders across whole industries, sectors, or nations to address the most audacious innovation challenges. Catalysts are also essential if we are to establish the global norms and guardrails that keep AI aligned with human interests. They can bring in overlooked voices, surface second-order effects, and help mitigate AI risks and share the benefits of AI more equitably. Many leaders must be prepared to reinvent themselves if they hope not only to generate AI innovative solutions but actually turn them into reality—launch or commercialize them so they have the desired impact. They must give up being the smartest person in the room and get comfortable collaborating, experimenting, and learning with “unusual suspects.” Even AI-first executives admit we do not know where AI will take us nor how we are going to get there. The winners of the AI race will be the companies whose leaders accept that their task is wayfinding. Leaders must enable their colleagues to have the courage and curiosity of explorers who once crossed the uncharted oceans without clarity about their destination. Fortunately, we have access to more sophisticated tools than the ancient seafarers. For AI to make a positive difference for customers and society, it will take individuals at all levels of our organizations to be willing and able to join the journey. TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

Exclusive: Obscure Committee Set To Be at Center of Trump Fight if Democrats Win House
2026-09-08

Exclusive: Obscure Committee Set To Be at Center of Trump Fight if Democrats Win House

TIME | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Chairman Bruce Westerman, R-Ark., speaks as ranking member Jared Huffman, D-Calif., listens during a House Natural Resources Committee markup on Tuesday, May 6, 2025. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings If Democrats win control of the House in November, party leaders plan to turn the final two years of the Trump era into a barrage of investigations over alleged corruption and contempt for the law. Much of the scrutiny would be expected to come from the Judiciary and Oversight Committees, the traditional engines of congressional investigations. But another, less prominent committee could emerge as an unlikely center of the fight: the House Natural Resources Committee. The 45-member panel has oversight over virtually all federal lands, giving it jurisdiction over a striking collection of controversies that have defined President Trump’s second term. That includes his efforts to remake Washington, D.C. by building a White House ballroom and a triumphal arch, and the troubled renovation of the Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool. It also covers the Administration’s efforts to open up national parks to an unprecedented amount of hunting, fishing and drilling, as well as a recent proposal to give a swath of California’s Yosemite National Park to a private developer. “We are no longer a backwater here at the Natural Resources Committee,” Rep. Jared Huffman, the committee’s top Democrat, tells TIME. “You check a lot of boxes with the Natural Resources Committee in terms of some of the most high-profile controversies in this Administration.” Huffman, who is likely to become the committee’s chair in a Democratic House, spoke to TIME this month from his Capitol Hill office decorated with paintings from the Muir Woods National Monument, the Golden Gate National Recreation Area, and other public lands in his coastal California district. Leading the House Natural Resources Committee has been the ultimate goal for the former environmental lawyer since he joined the House in 2013. “This is the top of the mountain for me,” he says. “This is what I always wanted.” Huffman has a running list of the investigations he would pursue if he takes the gavel. His plans would amount to a striking transformation for a committee more commonly associated with debates over endangered species, grazing rights, and federal water projects. Since Trump returned to office, committee Democrats have tried to investigate and call attention to Administration actions while lacking the authority to compel cooperation. With control of the House, those requests could be backed by subpoenas, sworn depositions, and public testimony from Administration officials, contractors, and private companies. Huffman says he expects a wave of government employees to approach the committee as whistleblowers, feeling more confident that Democrats will act on their concerns. “There are principled, patriotic Americans working in all of these federal agencies that are horrified with the corruption and illegality that’s been happening around them,” he says. “Some of them have already been helpful, but I think many, many more will come forward under a Democratic majority.” Read more: The Democrat Ready to Beef Up Pentagon Oversight if the House Flips The Trump Administration says the changes to federally controlled land are intended to make the District of Columbia “safe and beautiful” by restoring monuments, cleaning up federal parkland, and improving public safety. Under its current rules, the Natural Resources chairman has broad authority to issue subpoenas for testimony and documents. The precise scope of that position’s power in the next Congress would depend on the rules adopted by the House and the committee next year. The Trump Administration could also refuse to comply with some subpoenas, producing lengthy court battles. Huffman is perhaps best known on Capitol Hill as the founder of the Freethought Caucus, which counts 37 members and advocates for church-state separation and true religious freedom. Last month, he published a book, No Prophets: The Fight to Save Democracy from Christian Nationalism. But if he gets the gavel, his work investigating the Administration would shift to the forefront. Huffman says he intends for the committee to revisit many of the Administration’s decisions from the previous two years. “We will not only be doing the oversight that presents itself on our watch,” he says. “We will be doing two years of catch-up for a Republican Congress that looked the other way in the face of all this corruption, self-enrichment and illegality.” The committee’s most visible investigations could target what Huffman calls Trump’s “vanity projects” including the $400 million, 90,000 sq. ft. White House ballroom being built on the site of the demolished East Wing. Last month, the Supreme Court allowed the construction to move forward, after a lower court had halted the work saying Trump did not have the authority to build the ballroom without congressional approval. The President is also set to break ground on a 250-foot “triumphal arch” near the Arlington National Cemetery that would be taller than the Arc de Triomphe in Paris. He has also proposed erecting hundreds of statues in a Garden of American Heroes and transforming the East Potomac Golf Links into a championship-level course. Some of the projects have moved forward without congressional authorization or have been reviewed by commissions that Trump has filled with allies. The Administration has maintained that the work is legal and will restore grandeur to the capital. "It's a dramatic abuse of the public trust for one man's ego,” Huffman says of the projects. “It's also a direct attack on the rule of law because we have long had laws like the Commemorative Works Act that spell out in great detail how monuments and other structures in the nation's capital get authorized and built.” Huffman says that if Democrats win the House the committee would seek records showing how the projects were approved, how much public money had been spent, and whether donors received or sought favorable treatment from the Administration. The private financing, he says, would receive particular scrutiny. He wants to know who donated, how much they contributed and what business they had before the federal government. Last year, the White House released a partial list of ballroom donors, including crypto and tech billionaires and defense contractors, but has not revealed how much each donor is giving. Huffman also questioned why money and Park Service personnel were being concentrated in Washington while the Administration was cutting staff and services elsewhere in the national park system. “You could look at it and say, well, you know, why die on that hill? These are just little pet projects of Donald Trump,” Huffman says. “But this is real money that is being misprioritized. It’s an abuse of the public trust.” Another Washington project he wants to investigate is the renovation of the Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool. Trump ordered the pool’s bottom covered with a blue coating as part of a rehabilitation that cost more than $14 million. Soon after the project was completed, algae returned and sections of the new coating began to peel. The President blamed vandalism. In July, the Administration dropped a felony charge against a U.S. Olympic canoeist it had blamed for the damage, and acknowledged in court that the damage was “a result of flawed installation by the contractor." Huffman says he expects whistleblowers inside the Park Service to provide additional information about how the contracts for the pool project were awarded and supervised, when officials learned that the work was failing, and whether employees were pressured to misrepresent what had occurred. “The fact that National Park Service employees have been told to lie about what happened, for example, we obviously want a full accounting,” he says. (The White House did not respond to Huffman’s allegation that Park Service employees were instructed to lie). If federal agencies refuse to provide the committee with records, Huffman adds, he would be prepared to issue subpoenas. “You walk softly and carry a big stick,” he says. “The subpoena is your big stick. You hope not to use it, but I think it’s very good to have.” The recent bipartisan outcry over the prospects of a private developer gaining a swath of Yosemite National Park illustrates how the committee’s work could shift dramatically under Democratic rule. The current chairman, Republican Rep. Bruce Westerman of Arkansas, has not announced any committee action regarding the Administration’s proposal to a land swap intended to help Kingsbarn Realty Capital, a private real estate firm, build a road between property it owns outside Yosemite and roads inside the national park. The company plans an upscale development of cabins, homes, and commercial buildings on two parcels near the park’s western boundary. “I don’t like what it would do on its own terms to Yosemite,” Huffman says. “But I especially don’t like the precedent, because if they’re allowed to do it, this will be coming to a national park near you in some other form.” Under the proposal, the government would transfer a small parcel of park property in exchange for land of comparable value that Kingsbarn would acquire elsewhere in California. The company has argued that the shorter route would reduce driving and pollution. The Interior Department has said no decision has been made and that any exchange would have to comply with federal law, environmental reviews and public-notification requirements. It has denied reports that political appointees improperly pressured the Park Service to advance the proposal. Huffman says if he becomes Natural Resources chair, he intends to seek communications between the developer, the White House, Interior officials and Park Service employees. Investigators would look for evidence that political or financial ties influenced the process, including any connections between the company and Trump or his family. Campaign finance records show Kingsbarn’s chief executive, Jeff Pori, making more than $4,000 in contributions to Trump's campaign in 2024. The company’s representative, Lanny Davis, has said politics had nothing to do with the proposal. But Huffman remains skeptical.  “You’ve got to dig into the money trail,” Huffman says. “You’ve got to figure out who’s been going to Mar-a-Lago and paying tribute to the Trump family.” Huffman says he would also probe the Administration’s “war on clean energy,” including its terminating offshore wind leases and redirecting money toward fossil-fuel investments. Interior officials have defended the agreements as a way to dispose of projects they regard as unreliable and economically unworkable. Democrats have questioned whether the Administration had the authority to make the deals and whether taxpayer money or federal lease payments were improperly used. Other investigations would involve government investments, loans and equity stakes in mineral companies. Huffman says he would examine how companies were selected, whether federal safeguards were followed and whether Trump’s family or associates stood to gain financially. He adds that investigations could not consume the committee’s entire agenda. He plans to pursue legislation on environmental permitting, public lands and energy, as well as tribal water settlements, health care, education and public safety. Citing the committee’s preview over Native American land and communities, he says it may also examine prediction markets such as Kalshi, which tribal leaders contend are encroaching on gaming revenue used to finance government services. “It's going to be an enormously busy Congress,” Huffman says if Democrats win the House, as most polls suggest. “The whiplash when we go from this to what will be a very fast-moving and unrelenting workload demand—I mean, it's going to be a shock for a lot of members. It's going to challenge me. It's going to challenge all of us.”

Iran Threatens to Target U.S. Energy Assets in Gulf If U.S. Strikes Again

Iran Threatens to Target U.S. Energy Assets in Gulf If U.S. Strikes Again

TIME | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Men gather at the damage to a storage and warehousing facility at the port of Kuhestak following a U.S. strike in Iran's southern Hormozgan province on Sept. 6, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Iran warned on Monday that it could target U.S. oil and gas interests in the Gulf if the U.S. launches any new attacks on its assets. Responding to U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth’s warning that “if Iran shoots at U.S. ships, we will destroy (and sink) their oil tankers,” Iranian Parliament Speaker Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf said: “It’s simple: the oil and gas production chain here is sprawling, accessible, and exposed. American oil and gas companies across these waters and facilities share that exposure.” “Strike our assets and you get struck,” he added. “We’ve already proven it.” Ghalibaf’s warning comes as Tehran seeks greater leverage amid Washington’s ongoing economic pressure campaign and stalled efforts to negotiate an end to the war that the U.S. and Israel launched on Feb. 28. Mohsen Rezaei, Iran’s Supreme National Security Council Secretary, told Iranian state TV on Sunday that Tehran has concluded “new strategies must be adopted in the war, in negotiations, and in confronting the blockade.” Among the measures planned is a new “exclusion zone” in the Persian Gulf and Gulf of Oman. The zone would begin at the blockade line, extend through the Strait of Hormuz, and continue into the Persian Gulf, Rezaei said. Vessels entering the zone to transit the Strait would be placed on an Iranian sanctions list. The latest flare-up began Saturday, when Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) attacked U.S. warships in the region. The U.S. responded by striking three Iranian oil tankers, including one near Kharg Island, Iran’s main oil export hub. The U.S. and Iran have for the past week exchanged a flurry of attacks, ending a brief respite in active hostilities. Oil prices reached near six-week highs on Monday as commercial traffic through the Strait of Hormuz, a narrow waterway through which a fifth of global oil and liquefied natural gas shipments flowed before the war, has fallen sharply. Diplomatic efforts between the U.S. and Iran have stalled. The two countries signed a Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) on June 17, agreeing to reopen the Strait of Hormuz and engage in technical talks to end the war. But the truce was short-lived. After a commercial vessel was struck in the Strait in late June, the U.S. attacked Iranian missile storage and radar sites, prompting Iran to retaliate against U.S. military installations in the region. Intermittent attacks continued in July, and fighting resumed in late August after a brief, informal pause. The Trump Administration has said it is investigating a U.S. strike last week on a family compound in southern Iran where a wedding celebration was underway, killing at least five people and wounding more than 60, according to Iranian state media. No new formal negotiations have been announced since the MOU’s 60-day deadline expired on Aug. 17. Although some of Iran’s top officials, including President Masoud Pezeshkian, have signaled a willingness to return to the MOU and resume talks with the U.S., the two countries’ positions remain far apart. The U.S. wants to significantly restrict or dismantle Iran’s nuclear program and guarantee unrestricted commercial passage through the Strait of Hormuz. Iran has called for broad sanctions relief, access to frozen assets, an end to the U.S. naval blockade of Iranian ports, and a role in managing the Strait. Hard-liners in Iran have shown little appetite for compromise on those red lines, and analysts previously told TIME that Iranian leaders may believe time is on their side, with Tehran prepared to absorb further economic and military pressure in hopes that rising energy prices will eventually prove too politically costly to U.S. President Donald Trump. “The Iranian nation accepts neither threats nor coercion, nor negotiations whose outcome would be the weakening of the country’s rights,” Hossein Taeb, head of the IRGC’s Basij paramilitary force, said on Monday. “Negotiations are for securing the nation’s rights, not surrendering them.” While Trump faces domestic pressure to rein in gas prices and avoid an open-ended war in the Middle East ahead of the November midterms, he has also appeared determined to secure an outcome he can present as a victory. “I’m not trying to force Iran to the bargaining table,” Trump posted on Truth Social last week. “I like our position now much better, with almost total control of the Hormuz Strait, and their economy totally collapsing. They are just playing out the inevitable.” Iran is planning to establish a new restricted zone to amp up global economic pressure and force an end to the U.S. blockade. At the same time, Iran and Oman have agreed on a temporary shipping corridor through the Strait of Hormuz and are in talks on its future administration—an arrangement that could formalize Tehran’s role in managing the waterway against U.S. opposition. But Ghalibaf acknowledged in parliament on Sunday that Iran is struggling with sharp inflation, a collapsing rial, and high unemployment. Washington’s economic pressure campaign, which includes the naval blockade, sanctions on Iran, and the threat of secondary sanctions on Iran’s trading partners, has squeezed Tehran. The International Monetary Fund estimates that inflation in Iran is near 70%. The Strait of Hormuz, which Iran began restricting at the start of the war, has been the country’s most important bargaining chip. But that leverage may be weakening as Gulf states turn to routes that bypass the Strait and the U.S. escorts shipments through the corridor. The conflict has also inflicted mounting costs on Iran’s own economy by trapping tens of millions of barrels of Iranian crude inside the Gulf.

The AfD Is Rising. Germany’s Political Center Is Failing.
2026-09-07

The AfD Is Rising. Germany’s Political Center Is Failing.

TIME | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Leaders of the far-right Alternative for Germany party celebrating first exit poll win to AFD following state elections in Saxony-Anhalt on September 6, 2026 in Magdeburg, Germany. An electoral earthquake has fractured Germany’s societal and political tectonic plates. In a state election on Sunday, the far-right Alternative for Germany party won 43.8% of the vote in Saxony-Anhalt, a state of roughly two million people in the eastern part of the country. When Friedrich Merz first campaigned to lead Germany’s conservative Christian Democratic Union (CDU), in 2018, he vowed to reduce support for the far-right AfD in half. In a bitter irony, Chancellor Merz’s CDU lost half its support in Saxony-Anhalt and after its resounding victory the AfD has become a direct threat to his leadership, and is beginning to overturn Germany’s postwar political order. The drubbing in Saxony-Anhalt may be only the beginning. Two more regional elections are scheduled later this month and could demonstrate the growing strength of Germany’s fringe parties and intensify pressure on Merz’s governing coalition in Berlin. The AfD is more than a political disruptor. German domestic intelligence has classified parts of the party, including its Saxony-Anhalt chapter, as right-wing extremist. Yet the AfD has overtaken the CDU in national polls, while Merz’s approval ratings have fallen to levels unprecedented for a German chancellor. Although Merz has backtracked on campaign promises, most notably his commitment to uphold the debt brake—loosening the constitutional limit on government borrowing—he has remained resolute in preserving the “firewall” between the CDU and AfD. That firewall is intended to keep Germany anchored in its postwar democracy deliberately constructed with guardrails against its darkest history to prevent backsliding into authoritarianism. The AfD has used Merz’s flip flop on the debt brake against him and is questioning defense spending on Ukraine. Upon taking office in May 2025, a little over a year ago, Merz promised to revive Germany’s stagnant economy and increase defense spending to position the country as Europe’s de facto military leader. An antagonistic Russia and an unreliable America have left Germany with few alternatives. German businesses are suffering with high costs, American tariffs and intensifying competition from China. Merz also made another defining promise: to beat back the AfD and restore the centrist CDU’s  claim to being the true conservative party in Germany. Instead, the election in Saxony-Anhalt will amplify calls, including from within his own party, for him to step aside. Merz’s blunders over the past year cannot be dismissed as the mistakes of a political novice. He is a seasoned operator who, unlike many of his German peers, has extensive experience in the private sector. He promised an ambitious program of reforms to tackle Germany’s welfare and pension programs, but his proposals were too tepid and too late. And his poor communication style has also overshadowed his efforts on curbing migration and his diplomatic campaign across Europe to sustain support for Ukraine. The CDU’s predicament is not surprising. Merz has never been a particularly beloved figure, and the grand coalition between the conservatives and the Social Democratic Party was hardly the outcome German voters desired after the federal election last year. After the Federal Republic of Germany was established in 1949, there had been only  single Grand Coalition government during the first four decades. But Merz’s government is the fourth Grand Coalition government in Germany in the last two decades. This coalition, was supposed to be different: it was formed to enact far-reaching reforms and hold the AfD at bay. So far, it has done neither. The AfD began in 2013 as a movement of anti-euro skeptics. It has since become a nationwide force, building its constituency through relentless opposition to immigration, nativist appeal and efforts to relativize Germany’s Nazi past. An anemic German economy and frustration with the established parties have accelerated its rise. The AfD is now the leading political party in Germany with support from nearly one-third of German voters. Like its far-right counterparts elsewhere in Europe, the AfD has prospered by channeling voters’ anger and grievance. Until now, however, it has remained in opposition. No other German party has been willing to forge a parliamentary coalition with the AfD, and even some European far-right parties consider the AfD so radical that they prefer to keep it at arm’s length. The AfD’s victory in Saxony-Anhalt and other upcoming regional elections could change that dynamic. Although the CDU and the AfD hold overlapping positions on migration and some social questions, the AfD’s hostility toward Germany’s culture of historical remembrance is fundamentally incompatible with the country’s center-right tradition. The CDU’s first leader, Konrad Adenauer, committed Germany to integration with the West—a principle known as Westbindung—that the AfD would dismantle. Merz has steadfastly defended that orientation. The AfD, by contrast, seeks renewed ties with Russia and Germany’s withdrawal from the euro, which it presents as a path to economic revival. The mood of the country can now be read most clearly in the regional elections, where voters are preoccupied with jobs, immigration, schools and public services. These are precisely the areas in which the grand coalition that Merz leads is coming up short. The AfD offers a story of national decline but few credible solutions and no meaningful experience of governing. Yet criticizing the AfD is not sufficient for the CDU. The democratic answer to the rise of the AfD must be responsible government, solving complex problems through the institutions and the practices of a healthy democracy. Germany’s response matters beyond its borders. Across the Atlantic world, Germany's confrontation with extreme nationalist movements is being observed through the light of its history. Germany stands as a reminder of how swiftly democratic norms can erode and an example of a nation that has spent eight decades trying to preserve the lessons of that collapse. Merz may not be able to revive his personal fortunes, but he can still shape his  legacy by reminding Germans of their particular duty to defend democracy. The most effective answer to the AfD is not moral condemnation alone but competent government and courageous leadership. Boldness may not win Merz a second term, but it could help arrest the rise of the AfD.

Trump Shares Map Renaming New Mexico as 'New America,' Days After 'Trump Strait' and 'Lake America' Uproar

Trump Shares Map Renaming New Mexico as 'New America,' Days After 'Trump Strait' and 'Lake America' Uproar

Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump boarding Air Force One ahead of his travels to New Jersey for the Labor Day weekend in Maryland, United States, on Sep. 4, 2026 Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump appeared to float the idea of renaming New Mexico as “New America,” sharing several maps of the state that listed the new moniker. The images posted Monday, without context or comment, came a day after Trump uploaded a "New America" map via social media—an item that was later reshared by the official White House account. Trump also posted an AI-generated video of himself slapping “America” over a road sign that had previously read “Welcome to New Mexico” and claimed that “many people” had suggested the name change. The White House has not announced any effort to rename New Mexico. TIME has reached out for comment. The rhetoric has prompted backlash from Democratic lawmakers representing the state. “New Mexico’s name isn’t up for debate—it’s been ours since before the United States existed,” said the state’s Gov. Michelle Lujan Grisham. “While the White House wastes time coloring on maps, Nuevo México is busy doing the work.” Lujan Grisham suggested that Trump’s renaming jibes were an attempt to “distract Americans from his disastrous war in Iran,” as well as the rising price of gas and other consumer goods. “You can’t be serious. New Mexico is not for renaming. Not up for sale,” said former Secretary of the Interior and candidate for state Governor Deb Haaland. “My promise: I’ll always stand up for what makes New Mexico, New Mexico.” Sen. Ben Ray Luján offered a similar rebuttal, insisting “it’s New Mexico, always has been and always will be” as he referred to Trump as “corrupt.” Elsewhere, some Trump allies came out in support of the floated name change. “New America? Kind of catchy,” argued Republican Sen. Mike Lee of Utah. The President’s social media rhetoric regarding New Mexico comes days after he floated renaming the vital trade passage the Strait of Hormuz as “Trump Strait.” It also follows Trump signing an Executive Order renaming Lake Ontario as “Lake America” on Aug. 27, amid a widening trade war with Canada. The order applies to U.S. federal references of the lake and does not determine how Canada—or others—refer to the body of water. U.S. users of Google Maps and Apple Maps, however, will notice the change reflected. The change was met with widespread disapproval from both U.S. lawmakers and Canadian politicians, who vowed Lake Ontario will continue to be called by that name. An Ipsos/Reuters poll of the American general population, conducted Aug. 28-30, found that 63% of respondents opposed Trump's renaming of Lake Ontario. Trump has often turned to name changes as a show of power and a way to undermine his perceived adversaries. After returning to the White House last year, Trump signed an Executive Order renaming the Gulf of Mexico as the “Gulf of America.” The name has since been used by federal agencies and departments to refer to the sea south of the U.S. The President has also put his stamp on Washington, D.C., renaming the Kennedy Center by adding his own surname to the title. After much back-and-forth and legal complications, the Center’s board voted in August to reinstate Trump’s name on the building’s facade, testing a judge’s order from May that mandated its removal. Trump added his name to the U.S. Institute of Peace late last year, pointing towards his claim of ending a number of global conflicts since his return to the White House. Outside of renaming landmarks and buildings after himself, Trump has also faced backlash over a string of what Democratic lawmakers have referred to as “vanity projects” across D.C.
