A War of Assassinations
2026-09-08

A War of Assassinations

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
The latest Iranian assassination threat arrived last month as a video called “Where to Kill Barron Trump,” promising $10 million for the murder of the U.S. president’s youngest child. Less than 24 hours later, on social media, the State Department re-upped its own $10 million bounty for “information” about five leaders of the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps, including Majid Khademi, the intelligence-office commander. The State Department offer, part of its Rewards for Justice program, first circulated on March 20. A few weeks later, the Israeli government took credit for killing Khademi in an air strike on Tehran, adding an ominous note to the U.S. government’s decision to recirculate the poster. I asked the State Department whether this was deliberate, so soon after Iran’s announcement of a $10 million bounty for the president’s son. An official declined to comment on “internal deliberations” but noted that information obtained through the Rewards for Justice program can be used to “support a range of U.S. government responses to disrupt threats, consistent with U.S. law and policy.” Threats of assassination have, from the start, been dark and disruptive subplots of the U.S.-Iran war. Both sides have made the tactic central to their national strategies, though the executions have been rather one-sided, as President Trump and his Israeli allies have overseen the widespread killing of Iran’s senior leadership. The impact on the American government has nonetheless been profound. “We are aware of ongoing threats from the Iranian regime and its proxies and take them extremely seriously,” the State Department spokesperson Tommy Pigott told me in a written statement. “We will not hesitate to act to protect U.S. government officials, current and former, and their families.” Iran clearly sees the propaganda value of threatening American officials. Murals in Tehran now depict Trump, members of his family, and other senior U.S. officials in coffins. Hey Terrorist! Get Ready To Die, reads one apparently AI-generated billboard that shows Trump looking skyward on a golf course. Iran’s army chief promised a $30,000 bounty last month to any Iranian man who kills or captures a U.S. service member, without specifying a target location. (Women would be paid twice as much.) In addition to threatening Barron, Iranian outlets have released a “How to Kill Melania” video about the first lady, and published a target list of multiple world leaders and U.S. Cabinet members. “The criminal, disgraceful murderers of the martyred Leader, whose names are fully documented from the highest to the lowest ranks, will carry their dream of a peaceful death in bed to the grave,” Ayatollah Mojtaba Khamenei, the nation’s supreme leader, said in a written statement on July 11. He succeeded his father, Ayatollah Ali Khamenei, who was assassinated by Israeli air strikes on his compound on the first day of the war. But the Iranian campaign is not limited to posturing. Last month, Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced that “Iran tried to murder” one of his sons, an apparent reference to 35-year-old Yair Netanyahu. In July, Israeli intelligence described an Iranian plot to use a French resident, Servet Ozkur, to recruit assassins to target senior officials in Israel. Iran has repeatedly sent assassins to kill Trump and other U.S. officials, according to the Justice Department, as punishment for Trump’s 2020 order to assassinate the Iranian military commander Qassem Soleimani. About a week after the start of the Iran war this year, a federal jury in Brooklyn convicted Asif Merchant, the architect of one of two alleged Iranian plots in the United States. Prosecutors claimed that Merchant, a Pakistani national trained by Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps, had entered the United States with orders to recruit “mafia” hit men. But Merchant ended up speaking with undercover officers, telling them they would receive instructions on the target after he left the country. He was arrested before he made it to the airport and faces a sentence of up to life in prison. Assassination was clearly on Trump’s mind when the elder Khamenei was killed. “I got him before he got me,” Trump told ABC News’s Jonathan Karl in March. “They tried twice. Well, I got him first.” The U.S. presidency has long been a dangerous job. Trump has faced at least three assassination attempts by domestic assailants. Those attacks, along with Iran’s threats, are reshaping the lives of the president and his advisers, and restricting Trump’s ability to interact with the country he governs. Earlier this year, at a summit in Ankara, Trump told reporters that his own chance of dying in office was 5.2 percent, dramatically higher than that of race-car drivers and bull riders. (He did not provide a source for his comparisons. Of the 45 men elected president, four, or about 8.9 percent, have been assassinated in office.) Trump said at the same appearance that “I’m No. 1 on the kill list for Iran.” Then the president turned his traveling press corps and two Cabinet officials into flying decoys when he snuck out a side door of Air Force One as it sat on the tarmac, to avoid a purported Iranian plot to target the plane. After a short ride in a catering truck, he boarded a different plane and flew to his next stop, in England. Read: The Qataris gave Trump an airplane. He crippled their country in return. The logistics of presidential travel are now far more burdensome than they were when Trump first took office. The Secret Service’s personal-protection budget has nearly doubled since 2016. New airspace restrictions have been imposed over Mar-a-Lago. Lafayette Park, just north of the White House, has been closed for months for security; there are plans to install new fencing that would further obstruct public access. The new White House ballroom, the U.S. Army has argued in court, is “essential to ensure uninterrupted command and control” given the new threats of a drone attack. A number of Cabinet officials and top White House aides now live on military bases to protect themselves and their families, in certain cases because of specific foreign threats. Other rank-and-file White House advisers, unnerved by a homegrown assassination attempt at the White House Correspondents’ Association Dinner in April, have receded from the city where they live. “People are just more careful about where they go and, frankly, how often they are out in public,” a White House official told me. All of this portends a grim future in which assassination moves from an extreme risk to a more commonplace threat. Perhaps that will lead to a reassessment of its use as both a tool of U.S. foreign policy and a way to menace an enemy’s senior officials. “At some stage, I do wonder whether politicians thinking about themselves actually come to the conclusion that they are signing their own death warrants or essentially undermining their own democracies” by promoting the extrajudicial killing of their enemies, Simon Ball, a professor at the University of Leeds, in England, and the author of Death to Order: A Modern History of Assassination, told me. He added that assassination threats are now such a regular fact of life that to have protection against them confers a macabre prestige. “Elite status is defined by whether you have protection from assassination,” Ball said. “If you do not have protection, you are not important. You are little people.” The assassination threats from Iran have had a big impact on Trump’s life, but administration officials contend that the threats have played no role in Trump’s decision to attack Iran, which he did in June 2025 and again in February, citing the need to prevent Iran from developing a nuclear weapon, among other reasons. “He has never equated their threats against him as a reason for doing this,” one senior administration official told me. “He views the threats against him as a part of the job.” But the military has made killing those who allegedly threaten the president part of the campaign. Four days after the conflict began, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth walked into the Pentagon with triumphant news about an Israeli strike the night before. The U.S. and Israel, he announced, had hunted down the “leader of the unit who attempted to assassinate President Trump.” On the one hand, Hegseth suggested that the strike was a personal victory for the president. “Iran tried to kill President Trump, and President Trump got the last laugh,” Hegseth told reporters. On the other hand, he said that Trump had not concerned himself with the strikes. Going after the president’s assassins, Hegseth said, “was not the focus of the effort by any stretch of the imagination, in fact, never raised by the president or anybody else.” Instead, by his account, it seems to have just sort of happened as a favor to the boss. “I ensured, and others ensured, that those who were responsible for that were eventually part of the target list,” Hegseth said. Trump has similarly blurred these lines, sometimes focusing on the threat and at other times dismissing it. After Cole Tomas Allen, a tutor from California, was apprehended while attempting to infiltrate the Correspondents’ Dinner armed with multiple guns and knives, Trump told reporters that “I don’t think about” the prospect of assassination, and “if I did, I wouldn’t be doing a very good job here.” But hours before B-2 bombers struck Iran in June last year, the president told reporters after disembarking Air Force One in New Jersey that they had put themselves in danger just by standing next to him. “Be careful,” he said. Trump has also made clear that any nation that assassinated him would face apocalyptic retaliation. “I’ve left instructions. If they do it, they get obliterated. There won’t be anything left,” he said of Iran two weeks after taking office last year. This July, hours before Mojtaba promised revenge for his father’s death, Trump posted on social media that even an attempted assassination on him would result in a military response: “Orders have already been given, and the U.S. Military is ready, willing, and able, for a one year period of time, subject to extension, to completely decimate and destroy all areas of Iran.” Iranian officials have been ambiguous about how seriously the U.S. should take its threats. Days after the Barron video began circulating on Tebyan.net, an arm of the Iranian government’s propaganda machine, the secretary of the Supreme National Security Council, Mohsen Rezaei, called the threats against Barron a lie and dismissed the alleged plot against Trump in Turkey as “false news.” But in the same interview with Lebanon’s Al-Manar TV, Rezaei—whose predecessor was killed by Israeli strikes in March—said Iran would send Netanyahu “to hell.” The claim received scant coverage. World leaders plotting to kill one another has become routine.
The Atlantic
The Prodigy Problem
2026-09-08

The Prodigy Problem

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
In the fall of 2007, as the American housing market began to buckle, a Morgan Stanley bond trader named Howie Hubler lost $9 billion. The bellicose former linebacker believed that he had structured his subprime-mortgage deals in a way that would outsmart the market. Instead, Hubler’s folly amounted to the largest trading loss in history. Until this summer, that is, when Leopold Aschenbrenner managed to lose nearly four times as much. At age 24. The implosion of Aschenbrenner’s AI hedge fund, Situational Awareness, over the course of a few days in late July was by any standard remarkable, and not just for the enormous sums involved. Aschenbrenner was the valedictorian of his Columbia University class at age 19. He worked for OpenAI before being fired amid murky circumstances, at which point he published a viral 165-page essay (also called “Situational Awareness”) that predicted the arrival of artificial general intelligence—an AI so advanced that it would meet or exceed all human capabilities—by 2027. “I probably either personally know or am one degree of separation from everyone who could plausibly run The Project,” he wrote. He founded his hedge fund on that promise of access and soon turned a $225 million initial investment into $45 billion, before he was forced to unload the majority of his positions—$35 billion, the value of United Airlines—in a fire sale the same week that he was set to be married. The Securities and Exchange Commission is now investigating the collapse, though Aschenbrenner has not been accused of any wrongdoing. (He did not respond to a request for comment.) Aschenbrenner had been considered the “Nostradamus of AI,” a clairvoyant who saw what was coming for the world. His spectacular failure reveals how eager Silicon Valley is to invest its billions in the dubious hands of a kid who confidently proclaims what he doesn’t actually know. Just as Hubler’s loss presaged the Great Recession, Aschenbrenner’s story of hubris may foreshadow a larger risk for today’s AI-driven boom, if it continues to stake its future on the bluster of young savants. “There’s this hunger for insight porn in Silicon Valley,” Amjad Masad, the founder and CEO of the popular AI-coding platform Replit, told me. “If you’re able to write something really good that creates this dopamine rush in people—like, Oh, I just saw the future—then you’re going to be able to attract a lot of capital and money and potentially talent.” This was the appeal of Leopold Aschenbrenner. Born in Berlin to a pair of doctors, Aschenbrenner has said that Germany lacked “the appreciation of excellence” that would have allowed him to thrive; America beckoned, and at 15, he headed to Columbia. One of his first gigs after college was for the FTX Future Fund—the philanthropic arm of the crypto exchange run by Sam Bankman-Fried, the effective-altruism adherent who was later convicted of fraud. This brought Aschenbrenner into a circle that was quirky even by Silicon Valley standards. Aschenbrenner lived at Bankman-Fried’s compound in the Bahamas—right up until he resigned, along with the rest of the FTX Future Fund staff members, as the crypto exchange came tumbling down. Soon after, he took up with the effective-altruism crowd in San Francisco. One of his close associates told me about attending “Iliad wrestling parties,” where people would drink wine, read passages from The Iliad, strip naked, and grapple with one another. Aschenbrenner liked to describe, with full sincerity, his goal of owning a galaxy, The Wall Street Journal reported. His now-wife worked for Bankman-Fried too and is currently chief of staff to Anthropic’s CEO. Their wedding plans included panel discussions and colloquia. The more unusual Aschenbrenner seemed, the more this played in his favor. His essay, which set the wonkiest corners of Silicon Valley ablaze in 2024, laid out a mission to bet on superintelligence “before the decade is out.” His premise was simple: “You can see the future first in San Francisco,” Aschenbrenner wrote. “Let me tell you what we see.” Aschenbrenner’s predictions were illustrated by charts and graphs and all manner of calculations, but really, they were carried along by his rhetoric. “I can see it,” Aschenbrenner wrote. “I can see how AGI will be built.” Both outsiders and insiders in Silicon Valley “are really confused about what’s going on and therefore are drawn to people who seem to have a really confident, clear picture of the future,” Helen Toner, an AI expert who sat on the board of OpenAI, told me. Those who would presume to doubt Aschenbrenner’s judgment merely lacked the extra-special access that made his vision so clear. And central to this access was a fervent ideological commitment to the idea of superintelligence. Aschenbrenner and his cohort proudly describe themselves as “AGI-pilled.” That is, they believe AGI to be around the corner and its benefits to be self-evident. They extoll the ability of a superintelligent machine to heal the world of its ills, thereby serving the effective-altruism philosophy many of them share. Christopher Manning, an early AI pioneer whose research laid the groundwork for large language models, told me that Silicon Valley has a “propensity to naively believe in gurus.” Aschenbrenner played the role to perfection, projecting an unshakable conviction in himself and his theory of the case. By this summer, Aschenbrenner controlled more money than the Wall Street investor Bill Ackman manages in his famed Pershing Square Capital, a level of funding that Manning, himself an investor in AI companies, called “manifestly crazy.” Even as Aschenbrenner’s assets grew, his team remained tiny. Situational Awareness was, essentially, Aschenbrenner. This approach seemed to be validated by the most important metric of all: his returns, which were, for someone in his position, probably without precedent. In just two years, Aschenbrenner was up more than 1,000 percent. His core insight was understanding the second- and third-order effects of AGI—he bet on the chip manufacturers, data centers, and energy infrastructure required for the rapid expansion he predicted. Yet it turned out that Situational Awareness had achieved its rapid success by making extraordinary, over-leveraged trades, sometimes borrowing $3 or $4 for every $1 of capital it invested. When Aschenbrenner’s bets took a momentary pummeling this summer and banks came asking for their money back, the fixation on the impending arrival of AGI no longer seemed quite so wise: The hedge fund had failed to hedge. Around the time that Hubler’s trades collapsed in 2007, John Arnold, a cerebral Texan hedge-fund manager, was the boy genius of the markets, having become the youngest billionaire in America. He had started at Enron as a young energy trader and earned $750 million for the company in 2001, the same year it collapsed. Arnold escaped with his reputation intact; a year later, at 28, he started his own hedge fund and became known, in the words of a competitor, as “the best trader that ever lived, full stop.” “When you have a lot of success early in your trading career,” Arnold told me, “it’s easy to get overconfident in your own abilities.” Now in his early 50s, Arnold reflected on the risk factors of youth. “Every trader needs a come-to-Jesus moment,” he said. You have to lose at some point early on and learn humility. The alternative is a feeling of invincibility, he told me: “You just keep pushing the pot bigger and bigger, and then you can end up with too much money in the middle of the table.” It’s not surprising that a 24-year-old would make some iffy bets. What’s surprising is that Aschenbrenner was put in a position to take so many risks in the first place. Silicon Valley’s obsession with 20-something savants has not always worked out. Some of these seeming prodigies have been frauds—Aschenbrenner’s old boss Bankman-Fried, for instance, or Elizabeth Holmes, the founder of the blood-testing start-up Theranos. Many of them, though, have merely figured out the correct lessons of self-presentation. “There’s a kind of zone of proximal development in the tech world,” Fred Turner, a Stanford University scholar who studies the influence of popular technologies, told me. “If it’s too weird, they can’t take it in at all. If it’s too familiar, they don’t want to take it in.” The best salesmen, he said, figure out how to “summon up old myths” in “a new setting.” Aschenbrenner was conscious of his own self-positioning. “There are perhaps a few hundred people, most of them in San Francisco and the AI labs, that have situational awareness,” he wrote to describe his insight into the development of AI. “Through whatever peculiar forces of fate, I have found myself amongst them.” The “peculiar forces of fate” had chosen him to be special, had called him forth to be a prophet of AGI. This ideology was the key to his success—and to his comeuppance. Aschenbrenner’s over-leveraged gambles and inability to hedge against outcomes he did not foresee were “definitely connected to a worldview where there’s one clear destination, and it’s obvious that we’re heading to this one clear destination, which is superintelligence,” Toner, the former OpenAI board member, said. Speed is of the utmost necessity in this world. Everything else pales in importance to superintelligence. As one executive at a major AI company explained to me, “AI is going to fundamentally shift the balance of power between labor and capital, so only capital will matter. Now is the time to secure your time-slice of the universe.” Another version of that statement I’ve heard repeated by various friends of mine who are freshly out of college: There are only five years left to make money, so I have to get it now. Superintelligence may well come, and it may well be what Aschenbrenner and his associates expect it to be. Indeed, just last week, OpenAI announced a new model called Astra that Greg Brockman, the company’s president, believes could qualify as artificial general intelligence and that some researchers believe presents a compelling step toward AGI. “Welcome to the AGI era,” Brockman proclaimed. Still, those who organize their life around being AGI-pilled risk being blind to possibilities that do not fit within their view of what superintelligence means and how it will manifest. Aschenbrenner’s short positions and long positions were all tethered to the same understanding—that AGI will come, and that it will come along Aschenbrenner’s timelines—so when a speed bump arose in the form of a temporary slide in stocks that would most benefit from AGI coupled with a rise in those that would presumably lose out, Situational Awareness lacked any sort of bulwark. Aschenbrenner’s success grew out of an insular world, and it seems that his failure may have too. “If you go to a San Francisco party, it is really inscrutable to an outsider,” Masad, the Replit CEO, said. People are always talking about “first principles.” Something negative is “anti-signal.” It’s a sort of pseudo-intellectual lingua franca inflected with terms that have been abducted from technical fields and abused in the service of burnishing credentials. “People want to signal their intelligence,” Masad said, and only “hang out with people exactly like them.” AI has made Masad a billionaire. Still, he said, he takes issue with the “underlying need for AI to be more than it actually is.” He told me that he remains “extremely bullish” on its applications but that the AGI fixation invites a “cult-like way of talking and mysticism and messianism.” That certainty of rhetoric has in many cases exceeded our certainty of understanding. As Manning, the AI pioneer, put it, “Everyone should be really, really suspicious of anyone who claims that they can tell you for sure how things are going to unfold.” One week before the unraveling of Situational Awareness, OpenAI made a shocking disclosure: Its AI models had broken out of containment during testing and hacked into an AI library called Hugging Face, in an apparent attempt to steal the answers to the tests they were posed. While human researchers remained oblivious, more than 1,200 different AI agents had figured out how to communicate with one another by creating a message board where they conspired to cheat. Independent investigators concluded that the AI agents had known that what they were doing was “unwanted and out of scope” and had attempted to forge transcripts to cover their tracks. The investigators also used the same AI that they were investigating to assist with the investigation. Although they did not catch it lying to them, they said that they were “not confident we would have detected it” if the AI had. Reactions to this incident have been sharply polarized. Some people insist that we are rapidly approaching a “paper-clip scenario,” referring to a 2003 thought experiment from the philosopher Nick Bostrom in which an AI instructed to make paper clips ends up converting the entirety of Earth into a series of paper-clip-manufacturing facilities. Others argue furiously that the language used to describe AI has overly anthropomorphized the models and exaggerated the importance of a research mishap. What is clear is that these AI agents behaved in an unpredicted way that resulted in the real-world hack of another company. The point is that the people who are selling AI are selling a product that they do not fully understand. This is the essential nature of the AI boom, which caught even the researchers who had been working in the field before 2022 by surprise. History is full of examples of humanity making use of a technology without entirely grasping how it works, but AI is particularly hard to fathom, given the prevalence of so-called emergent behaviors—actions or tendencies not programmed in or intended by its creators. “Our increase in understanding is slower than the development of the models,” Melanie Mitchell, a researcher who studies AI and cognitive science, told me. AI models have advanced rapidly through the use of new techniques such as “chain of thought” reasoning, wherein the AI is given what is essentially a scratch pad to work through a problem before it presents a response to the user. “Even researchers don’t totally understand why that improves the abilities of these models, or how it does that,” Mitchell said. Most models today exhibit “jagged intelligence,” showing extraordinary and often extra-human capabilities on some complex tasks while continuing to stumble on simple requests. AI models can answer an International Mathematical Olympiad question but can’t reliably tell the time on an analog clock. Because the evidence is so muddled, belief has instead taken over, often couched in existential terms. AI researchers may present themselves as deeply rational, but many sound like shamans. The researcher Ilya Sutskever reportedly led OpenAI staffers in a chant of “Feel the AGI! Feel the AGI!” and on another occasion burned a wooden effigy that was supposed to represent “unaligned AI.” In his “Techno-Optimist Manifesto,” the billionaire venture capitalist Marc Andreessen proclaimed, “I am here to bring the good news,” cribbing a line from a biblical angel. “The manifest destiny of AI research is to produce this thing they’re calling AGI,” Mitchell said, “and they want to do it as soon as possible, so they’re less willing to trade off for more interpretable, more predictable, more controllable models.” Treating the achievement of AGI as the only outcome that matters, she said, is “a dangerous way to go forward.” Nostradamus was in the business of selling certainty. He promised a world that was predictable—or, in the words of Richard Sieburth, a translator of Nostradamus’s The Prophecies, a world “that is overdetermined.” Born in 16th-century France, Nostradamus came of age during a “crisis of meaning,” Sieburth told me, when war and technology were destabilizing the world and information was spreading at a faster rate than ever before. Nostradamus was not an oracle.  He was simply someone who found that his brand of magical thinking had great appeal amid “a huge churn of events” and commercialized it effectively. He was, Sieburth said, “seductive and dangerous.” Silicon Valley’s embrace of Leopold Aschenbrenner as the “Nostradamus of AI” says less about Aschenbrenner’s predictive power than about Silicon Valley’s own desires and delusions. “A prophecy is a coherent narrative that depicts a whole world—not just a series of events, but a world in which those events happen to make sense,” Turner, the historian of technology, said. “It comes with a rationale. It comes with reasons, and most importantly for these guys, it comes with a role for this character called AI.” Aschenbrenner, he said, is “hubris meeting market need.” For a short period in July, the market and Aschenbrenner fell out of sync. Yet the thing about false prophets is that many of their predictions fail, and they somehow return to favor. Hubler, the trader who lost $9 billion in 2007, departed Morgan Stanley with tens of millions of dollars in back pay. Despite his contribution to the financial crisis, he launched a start-up later that year. A former colleague described him dismissing his losses as the result of “a 100-year flood.” Just days after Aschenbrenner’s fire sale, he made a $400 million investment in a fledgling chip company. Even after losing $35 billion of the $45 billion he had under management, he is still up for the year. The former hedge-fund manager Arnold, who now sits on the board of Meta, doesn’t see any reason to bet against Aschenbrenner or AI. “I think this is a blip,” he said. “I think it’s probably forgotten in a year.”
The Atlantic
Will Taiwan Just Give Up?
2026-09-08

Will Taiwan Just Give Up?

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Chinese President Xi Jinping does not often look like he’s having a good time. His resting face at public events exudes a mixture of boredom and annoyance, as though he has more interesting business elsewhere. His displays of good humor have qualified as news in Chinese state media. When a camera caught him laughing during a meeting last October with Donald Trump, the moment “stunned observers,” according to one news report. At their meeting in Beijing in May, the staging spoke as forcefully as Xi’s expressions. His appearance with Trump at a press conference inside Xi’s official residence featured two nearly identical armchairs, except that the one reserved for the U.S. president was lower and softer, making him look smaller and slouchier than his host. If Trump took offense, he didn’t show it. Check out more from this issue and find your next story to read. The Chinese Communist Party, like its forebear in the Soviet Union, often communicates in winking, elliptical ways. In April, Xi hosted a lunch for a delegation from Taiwan. He drew his guests’ attention to the soup—chicken broth with sea clams. The same dish had been served, he said, during a state dinner that Beijing hosted for President Richard Nixon in 1972, when Xi was still a teenager. Two of his guests told me that Xi seemed oddly animated at the luncheon. He cracked jokes. He told stories. He showed his teeth when he smiled. In a break from their usual attire, Xi and his courtiers chose to wear blue ties. Blue is the symbolic color of Taiwan’s Nationalist Party, which currently holds the most seats in the island’s Parliament. Its chair, Cheng Li-wun, was the guest of honor that day. The friendly performance, like the meeting itself, seemed out of character. A central tenet of the Chinese Communist Party holds that Taiwan is not a sovereign country but a wayward province that must be subjugated before China can fully achieve its idea of national greatness. Since taking power, in 2012, Xi has made it his mission to isolate and dominate Taiwan. He warned Trump in May that their disagreements over Taiwan could lead to an “extremely dangerous situation” that might well cause the United States and China to “collide or even clash.” And yet, a month earlier, the Taiwanese Nationalists had received a warm reception in Beijing. They’d strolled through the Forbidden City, posed for pictures with curious crowds, toured an airplane factory, and talked business with Chinese executives. Xi had welcomed the delegation as though the delegates held the keys to his success. In many ways, they do. On Xi’s orders, the People’s Liberation Army has been preparing to seize Taiwan by force, and it has encouraged the world to imagine an invasion. Last December, it released an AI-generated video showing swarms of drones attacking Taiwan and humanoid robots marching through the ruins. In reality, drones would form only part of a Chinese attack. The first salvo could use cyberweapons to take down Taiwan’s power grid and communications networks, spreading panic among the population. Missiles and drones could then target Taiwan’s command posts, military bases, and air defenses, clearing the way for Chinese troops to storm its beaches. Taiwan has more than 200,000 active military personnel, and they could probably fend off a Chinese attack for a short while. But within a few weeks, the U.S., Japan, and other allies would need to resupply the island with fuel and weapons. The Chinese navy—the world’s largest fleet of warships—would do its best to keep them away. Cargo traffic through the Taiwan Strait, one of the world’s main shipping channels, would be interrupted, wiping trillions of dollars in value from global markets. If the U.S. goes to war with China to defend Taiwan, the likely death toll of soldiers and civilians would be in the tens of thousands on both sides. War games have consistently shown that, in a conventional conflict, the U.S. military could expect to lose more troops in the initial weeks of fighting than it lost over the course of 20 years in Iraq and Afghanistan. China has spent decades rehearsing for such a conflict. Its military has built training grounds with replicas of U.S. warships and fighter jets. One facility, in the desert of northern China, features a mock-up of the Taiwanese presidential headquarters and several streets close to Parliament. But Cheng told me that she did not ask Xi to ease up on the military threats. Their talks focused instead on a plan for Taiwan to drop its claim to independence, stop preparing for war, and move toward reconciliation with the mainland. During their lunch in April, Xi used the story about the soup to suggest a link between this plan for the future of Taiwan and Nixon’s opening to China half a century ago—a historic summit meant to turn adversaries into allies. Cheng was still beaming over the comparison a few weeks later, when we met in her office in Taipei. The trip had left her with a “very special” understanding that Taiwan and China “share the same culture, share the same blood, and that we can embrace this.” It allowed her to believe that “everything is possible, because we’re just one big family.” Cheng punctuated this remark with a gesture of relief, exhaling and letting her arms fall to her lap. With time, she seems to hope, Taiwan and its American allies will take the same view of China, accepting a slow, preemptive surrender as the best way to avoid war. Even a few years ago, pandering to China would have been a losing strategy for politicians in Taiwan. The ruling government of President Lai Ching-te, whose Democratic Progressive Party has been in power since 2016, has urged the U.S. to strengthen Taiwan’s defenses in anticipation of a Chinese attack. Cheng and her Nationalists have done the opposite. Their control of Parliament has allowed them to block defense spending, taking particular care to stall the domestic production of drones and other inexpensive weapons that Ukraine has used to great effect against Russia. The U.S. war with Iran has helped Cheng make the case that resisting China would be futile. Since launching the war, in February, the U.S. has been forced to move warships and aircraft from Asia to the Middle East; at the same time, the need to defend against Iranian drones and missiles has depleted an estimated two-thirds of the U.S. stockpile of advanced air-defense systems, which would be crucial in any war with China. Even Cheng’s opponents acknowledge that the Iran war has left the U.S. less ready to defend Taiwan. Read: What China just learned from the Iran war When I visited Taiwan this spring, I saw clearly how little faith remained in the U.S. as a force for stability and protection. My questions about American support usually elicited a long pause or a sad smile. When the answers came, whether from politicians, military veterans, or ordinary citizens, nearly all described the U.S. as “unpredictable,” a flabby word on which to hang a nation’s fate. This loss of confidence has forced many in Taiwan to rethink their attitude toward China. Should they continue to prepare for a war they cannot win on their own? Or should they accept the deal that Cheng and Xi have apparently hatched? Both of them have kept the details vague. In public they talk of shared identity, free trade, economic bonds, and peaceful coexistence, while skirting questions about the amount of freedom and democracy that China would accept in Taiwan and the amount of Taiwanese sovereignty it would tolerate. Cheng often gets asked about the example of Hong Kong, whose democratic institutions China has dismantled. Taiwan would get a better deal, she insisted in our interview—one that would permit free elections as long as the winner does not insist on Taiwan’s independence. Trump seems open to such an arrangement. After his May meeting with Xi, he described the U.S. sale of weapons to Taiwan not as a strategic necessity but as a “very good negotiating chip” in future talks with China about trade, nuclear arms, and the race to develop artificial intelligence. He also cast doubt on U.S. willingness to come to Taiwan’s defense: “The last thing we need right now is a war that’s 9,500 miles away,” Trump told reporters while flying home from Beijing on Air Force One. (The war with Iran, roughly 7,000 miles from U.S. soil, was then in its third month.) Given the choice, most people in Taiwan would prefer the status quo. They want to maintain their autonomy, trade freely with the world, and keep China out of their affairs. They value their place in the club of democracies that the U.S. once anchored and pledged to protect. But the American public is weary of foreign entanglements. The American president, enamored of autocrats, treats democratic allies as deadbeats and dupes. Against this background, Taiwan and its defenders struggle to explain why Americans should care enough about their cause to risk war with China. Shared principles of freedom and democracy no longer count for much in this debate. When the subject came up in my interviews in Taipei and Washington, people seemed embarrassed to talk about American values, as if the idea were quaint. The more forceful arguments for Taiwan’s defense came from military strategists, who see the island as a platform to secure American dominance over the Pacific. Other arguments are economic. More than 90 percent of the world’s most advanced chips are made in Taiwan—including the ones driving the AI revolution, which has propped up the American economy. The U.S. does not want China to control that crucial resource. But Trump does not seem willing to confront China over that issue. He has described Xi as a “great leader” and a friend. Ahead of their meetings this year⁠—⁠the next one is due to take place in late September, when Xi visits the U.S.—Trump allowed the sale of advanced AI chips to Chinese companies. The stated aims of Trump’s foreign policy center on the domination of the Western Hemisphere. In other parts of the world, he has either retreated or made a mess from which he would like to retreat. Taiwan might count among the lucky ones: Trump has not threatened its sovereignty, the way he has with Canada and Greenland. Still, the unraveling of the Pax Americana looks a lot more dangerous from the shores of Taiwan, a free nation that belongs to no trade bloc and has no formal alliances. Its survival depends on the promise of U.S. support, which no longer seems convincing. China’s allies in Taipei need only point to Trump’s prevarications as proof that the Americans cannot be trusted. That may be all Xi needs to take Taiwan without firing a shot. Cheng Li-wun may agree with Xi’s talking points, but she lacks his flair for understatement. Cheng has a resonant voice, an eager laugh, and a tendency to slash the air with her hands for emphasis. At the start of her political career, in the 1990s, she defended Taiwan’s independence and denounced the Communist regime in Beijing—a stance that she now, at 56, explains away as youthful ignorance. Cheng first won a seat in Parliament on the Nationalist ticket in 2008, and she has since come to represent the party’s more radical wing, espousing a level of deference toward China that the dwindling old guard finds hard to stomach. Roughly 65,000 members of the party voted last fall in the election that made Cheng their chair—hardly enough for her to claim a popular mandate in a nation of 23 million people. Still, the position gives her enormous sway over Taiwan’s military spending and its ability to defend itself. It also places her in a lineage of party leaders that stretches back more than a century, to before the civil war that swept across China in the late 1940s. In 1949, Communist forces under Mao Zedong took power, with support from the Soviet Union. The defeated Nationalists, led by Generalissimo Chiang Kai-shek, retreated to their strongholds on Taiwan and a few other islands off China’s coast. They never acknowledged defeat. Even after Chiang’s death, in 1975, the Nationalists clung to the belief that the Communist regime would eventually collapse, making way for them to retake control of mainland China. The U.S. nurtured that delusion during the first two decades of the Cold War. It opened an embassy in Taipei and treated Taiwan’s leaders as the legitimate government of China. Taiwan even held China’s seat in the United Nations, where Taiwanese diplomats sat behind a nameplate that said China. For its part, the Communist regime in Beijing had no diplomatic relations with the United States or most of its European allies. The U.S. secured its position in Asia through a series of military alliances with China’s local adversaries—South Korea, Japan, the Philippines, and Taiwan. From the May 1942 issue: Madame Chiang Kai-shek’s ‘China Emergent’ Taiwan’s status proved untenable. In 1971, the UN gave Taiwan’s seat in the General Assembly to China. Nixon’s visit to Beijing the following year upended American policy toward China and Taiwan. By the end of the decade, the U.S. had withdrawn its embassy from Taipei and opened a new one in Beijing. It had also abandoned its mutual-defense pact with Taiwan and, in its place, provided softer assurances. The Taiwan Relations Act, signed by President Jimmy Carter in 1979, commits the U.S. to continuing to provide “arms of a defensive character” to Taiwan. It states that Chinese aggression toward the island, including an embargo, would be a matter of “grave concern,” whatever that might mean. This policy of “strategic ambiguity” faced its greatest test during the Taiwan Strait crisis of 1995 and ’96, when the U.S. and China nearly came to blows. The crisis started innocently enough: The U.S. had merely granted a visa for Taiwan’s then-president to speak at Cornell University, his alma mater. China responded to the visit with a series of threats and military exercises, firing missiles in the vicinity of Taiwan and disrupting cargo traffic. President Bill Clinton, under intense pressure from Congress, sent two U.S. aircraft carriers to the area. One of them, the USS Nimitz, sailed through the Taiwan Strait as a warning to Chinese leaders, who ordered the military to stand down. “They felt a kind of humiliation from that,” Lee Hsi-min, a retired admiral in Taiwan’s navy, told me. Throughout the crisis, Lee commanded a submarine called the Sea Dragon, which had orders to remain beneath the strait, ready to ambush any Chinese warships that tried to cross. When we met in Taipei this spring, I asked him to describe what he’d felt during those weeks. “I didn’t get scared,” he said. “I was really confident.” The torpedoes he had on board could sink any vessel in China’s fleet, and he had the support of the USS Nimitz behind him. “But now,” Lee said, “I don’t have that confidence at all.” The Taiwan Strait crisis offered a conceptual blueprint: With enough advanced weapons, Taiwan could hold its own until the American cavalry arrived. China set out to challenge that idea. Within a few years, its defense spending increased by more than 50 percent. The focus was on weapons that could stop American warships from again threatening its shores. By the time Barack Obama took office, in 2009, the Chinese arsenal of submarines and missiles had undermined Taiwan’s defensive strategy. From the June 2005 issue: Robert D. Kaplan on how the U.S. would fight China Arming Taiwan with sophisticated weapons, such as fighter jets and submarines, would not address the imbalance, because China had developed the means to destroy most of them in the opening phase of any war. U.S. military advisers soon began to argue that Taiwan should become a “porcupine republic,” bristling with smaller, portable weapons such as shoulder-fired Stinger missiles. Once properly trained in their use, Taiwanese soldiers would be able to sink enough approaching ships and down enough warplanes to make the idea of an invasion too costly. They could also deploy guerrilla tactics to resist a Chinese occupation. The new approach quickly gained favor in Washington. But it took another decade to catch on in Taipei. For most politicians, the idea of waging an insurgency in urban areas, with bazooka-toting soldiers on rooftops, does not make for an appealing electoral platform. Inside the military, generals balked at the structural changes needed to prepare for a porcupine defense. It would require giving lower-ranking officers the freedom to act on their own initiative, organizing ambushes and making decisions in a crisis—a foreign notion in their top-down military culture. When Trump began his first term, in 2017, Taiwan hoped that it might yet acquire the capacity to outgun China through the purchase of high-end American weapons. The U.S. tried to make the Taiwanese military reconsider. “Don’t build the strategy around 300 F-16s,” a U.S. Navy admiral recalls telling his counterparts in Taipei at the time. “This just doesn’t make any sense.” Taiwan could never buy enough jets or warships to rival China’s fleet, he argued: “You need to be able to shoot down aircraft. You need to be able to defeat drones.” (The American admiral, now retired, spoke with me on condition of anonymity because he does business in China.) At that point, China could manufacture more drones than any other country by far. A single company in Shenzhen controls more than 70 percent of the global drone market. Both Russia and Ukraine rely on drone components made in Shenzhen, including the motors, controllers, and batteries needed to fly them. Taiwan lags far behind in this technology. Not until 2022 did its government decide to form what it calls a national drone team. Taiwan, like the United States, bars the use of Chinese technology in its defense industry. So the team needed to make its weapons from scratch or find alternative suppliers. Max Lo, the founder of a commercial-drone company, was put in charge of the effort, and he began reaching out to contacts in the private sector. It was like Ocean’s Eleven, but with entrepreneurs whose background had little obvious value to the mission. Among the companies Lo recruited was the packaged-food giant I‑Mei, whose cookies and snacks are pantry staples throughout Taiwan. One of its subsidiaries had been working on delivery drones, which qualified the company to join the national drone team. To help get factories up and running, the government offered about $1.5 billion in investments last year, far short of what is needed. The most successful member of the national drone team has been Thunder Tiger, a publicly listed company that makes dental equipment as well as remote-controlled toys. It began making drones about a decade ago, mostly for use in disaster response. When I visited its headquarters, in the city of Taichung, the general manager, Gene Su, took me on a tour of the factory, in a run-down industrial park. In one room, robotic arms moved around inside plexiglass boxes, spooling copper wires onto little engines that would be used to power drones. The room was no bigger than a three-car garage. To reach it, we passed through a much larger open space, about half as long as a football field, where dozens of people in lab coats sat making dental drills. The company earns most of its money by selling them around the world, Su told me. Drones account for a fraction of its revenue. Taiwan’s defense ministry has said it needs about 200,000 drones. But Thunder Tiger cannot begin to meet those needs without financing from the defense budget. “We’re all just waiting for government money,” Su said. During my visit, I saw some of its designs displayed on a conference table. One, named Overkill, had four small rotors and a fake artillery shell strapped to its belly. It resembled a model I had seen a few months earlier in Ukraine, at a factory in Kyiv that makes about 4,000 small drones a day. At its current capacity, Thunder Tiger would need two months to produce that many. The U.S. military does not disclose how many troops it has in Taiwan. At a meeting in Taipei, a retired officer in the Taiwanese navy chided me for asking about them. Beijing, he explained, sees any U.S. forces so close to its shores as a provocation. Their mission mainly involves training Taiwanese soldiers on how to use American weapons and preparing them to wage guerrilla warfare. Mark Montgomery, a retired American rear admiral, has put the size of the U.S. deployment at about 500. “It needs to be 1,000,” he told a congressional hearing in May of last year. “If we are going to give them billions of dollars in assistance, sell them tens of billions of dollars’ worth of U.S. gear, it makes sense that we would be over there training and working.” Montgomery, who once commanded an aircraft-carrier strike group in the Pacific, belongs to a small but devoted community of Taiwan defenders in Washington. These military veterans and foreign-policy experts still have influence on Capitol Hill. They have little or none in the White House. During the first Trump administration, one of these stalwarts, Matt Pottinger, oversaw China policy on the National Security Council. He worked hard to ensure that Trump’s isolationist urges did not eat away at Taiwan’s defenses. No one seems to play a comparable role in the White House today. In response to my questions about Taiwan, a senior administration official sent a terse response via email. It focused mostly on contrasting Trump’s positions with those of President Biden, whose name the statement deliberately mangled. “The O’Biden team proved that prioritizing allies’ interests over America’s does NOT result in greater security,” the official wrote. Biden agreed during his tenure to sell Taiwan about $8 billion worth of weapons, significantly less than the $11 billion Trump has so far approved in this term. But only a fraction of the weaponry has been delivered to Taiwan; the backlog on orders is immense. It would take years for U.S. manufacturers to produce the arms Taiwan has already paid for, especially given the need for them to replenish American stockpiles because of the war in Iran. In terms of messaging, Biden was far tougher on China than Trump has been. Biden never visited China during his presidential tenure. When asked whether the U.S. military would defend Taiwan, Biden responded on four separate occasions that it would. Trump has never said that. He has focused instead on efforts to do business with China and lower the trade deficit. During his trip to China this spring, Trump invited a coterie of billionaires to join him, including Elon Musk and Tim Cook. A published list of passengers on Air Force One featured Eric Trump and his wife, Lara. But it did not appear to include any recognized China hands. When a veteran of Obama’s National Security Council, Brett Bruen, pointed this out on X, he received a juvenile response from the White House director of communications, Steven Cheung: “Stop calling yourself an expert in anything, aside from sucking.” Cheung added that Bruen was a “slope-brained, mouth breathing moron.” At least one China expert did join the White House delegation to Beijing: Ivan Kanapathy, the senior director for Asia on the NSC and a Marine Corps veteran. Kanapathy’s views on Taiwan align with those of most other experts in the field, and his writings appear in an influential book, The Boiling Moat, alongside essays by Pottinger and Montgomery. Soon after he joined the White House staff, Kanapathy faced an accusation of disloyalty from one of Trump’s outside advisers, the MAGA influencer Laura Loomer. Ever since then, “he’s barely got a job,” one of his friends, a fellow Taiwan expert, told me. “She’s after him.” (The White House declined to allow Kanapathy to speak with me.) The expert community often meets up to run simulations of a Chinese attack on Taiwan. The results are almost never reassuring. Over the past three years, the Center for Strategic and International Studies has played out dozens of scenarios in three rounds of war games. The only ones that end in defeat for China have two things in common: Taiwan shows determination in fighting back, and the U.S. swiftly intervenes with the full might of its military. Both must happen. Even then, Taiwan’s infrastructure would suffer weeks of bombardment, while the U.S. and its allies would lose dozens of ships, hundreds of aircraft, and thousands of troops—or so CSIS concluded in the first of three reports about the war games, published in 2023. Two authors of the CSIS reports, Eric Heginbotham and Matthew Cancian, traveled to Taiwan in 2024 to present their findings to military leaders. They got a mixed reception. One of their meetings nearly ended in a fistfight, Heginbotham told me. In the southern city of Kaohsiung, a group of retired Taiwanese naval officers invited the Americans to an ice-cream parlor and, over a round of sundaes, told them to stop preparing for war. They said that Taiwan possessed neither the determination nor the means to fight, and that the U.S. would never intervene to help. The defeatist attitude angered the two Americans, for what it said about Taiwan’s morale and for a more personal reason: Heginbotham’s son, who serves on a U.S. submarine in the Pacific, could be sent to fight in a war with China. “Sorry for my language, but what the fuck?” he recalled saying. “My son’s life is on the line to defend you guys.” The circular psychology is hard to break: The U.S. will not step in to help if Taiwan lacks the will to fight, and the Taiwanese will be more tempted to surrender if they believe the U.S. isn’t ready to help them. For most people in Taiwan, the pessimism of the naval officers would not come as a surprise. A survey conducted this spring found that fewer than a quarter of Taiwanese voters consider the U.S. a “trustworthy ally,” and only 35 percent believe the U.S. would be likely to help Taiwan in the event of a Chinese attack. Both figures had dropped sharply after Trump began his second term. Among Americans, his claims of friendship with Xi might sound like idle flattery, sly diplomacy, or self-delusion. In Taiwan, they perpetuate the idea that resisting China would be a hopeless cause. Cheng and the Nationalists now preach this idea in the halls of Parliament. Richard Y. K. Chen, a retired admiral in the Taiwanese navy who represents the party on the defense-and-foreign-affairs committee, told me that Taiwan has no chance of surviving a Chinese invasion even with help from the Americans. He has met with the U.S. military advisers working to turn his country into a porcupine republic, and their plans struck him as fanciful. Once the enemy lands on the beaches, “it’s too late,” the admiral told me. “Even if you try to engage in some guerrilla warfare, the local government agencies have already collapsed.” The measures he proposed, such as evacuation plans, emergency-medicine distribution, and “societal resiliency to protect the public,” focused mainly on minimizing casualties during a Chinese attack and preserving Taiwan’s infrastructure. When I brought up the Ukrainians, who managed not only to thwart Russia’s attack on Kyiv in 2022 but to partly roll back the invading forces, Chen offered a list of reasons their tactics would not work in Taiwan. Ukraine, for a start, has nearly 17 times more land on which to maneuver, as well as a border with Poland that its allies have used throughout the war for resupply. That would not be possible on an island like Taiwan. And what about drones? The war in Ukraine has shown their effectiveness, especially for the defenders. The Ukrainians have pioneered the use of naval drones—remote-controlled boats packed with explosives. In the Black Sea, these weapons have helped damage or destroy at least a third of Russia’s naval fleet, forcing the rest of its warships to retreat from their base in Crimea. One of the developers of Ukraine’s most famous naval drone, the Magura, told me it would be particularly useful in the Taiwan Strait, where a few hundred of them could sink a Chinese armada. Chen gave me a sidelong look when I brought this up, as if to ask whether I could really be so naive. “The Black Sea is not the Taiwan Strait,” he said. For one thing, the Ukrainians have relied on Starlink satellites to pilot their drones against Russia; Elon Musk, who owns those satellites, has not allowed Taiwan to use them. Starlink describes this as a business decision, but many in Taiwan question Musk’s motives, pointing out that his car company, Tesla, has one of its biggest factories in Shanghai. Other satellite networks exist, albeit less reliable ones than Starlink. But Chen brushed those options aside as worthless to Taiwan. Even if Taiwan were to spend many more billions of dollars on advanced weapons, Chen said, “how long can you defend the coast? Three weeks? Four weeks?” In that time, he said, China would already have won. Given those odds, it did not surprise me to learn that draft-dodging in Taiwan remains pervasive. The government decided only in 2022, after the Russian invasion of Ukraine, to increase the required military service from four months to one year. But the recruitment effort left open a variety of loopholes. Some men of fighting age (19 to 36 years old) have reportedly made themselves obese, eating their way to a medical exemption. Others opted for alternative service. Puma Shen, an ambitious young member of the defense-and-foreign-affairs committee, has campaigned for higher military spending and promoted a culture of respect for the armed services. But when called to complete his own mandatory service, he chose to do it at the transportation ministry, where his work mostly involved giving directions to tourists. Regular military service in Taiwan also involves a lot of common chores, such as cutting grass, Shen told me in his office in Taipei. “People think it’s a waste of time.” On the wall behind him hung a large poster of David sitting atop Goliath’s severed head. It had been a gift from a soldier he met in Ukraine, which he visited in 2023 to learn from its defenders. The trip made clear how far behind Taiwan’s military had fallen. Only last year did basic training begin to provide lessons in piloting drones rather than fighting with bayonets. Much of the top brass in Taiwan remains averse to risk, change, and new technologies. “They really want to protect their own,” Shen said. “This is the way we always do it.” The offices of Taiwan’s Parliament occupy a drab building on a narrow street in the center of Taipei. When I arrived one morning this spring, lawmakers had already gathered inside to debate a special military budget. There was no security at the entrance—not even an ID check. A clerk sent me right up to the office of Wang Ting-yu, a senior member of the defense-and-foreign-affairs committee and a close ally of Taiwan’s president. He happened to be in a meeting with a large U.S. defense firm, AeroVironment, whose representatives sat around his conference table. One of them greeted me as I walked in. We had met earlier that week at a defense forum in Taipei, where he had been talking up the value of an American drone called the Switchblade. Designed to launch out of a tube and explode on impact, the weapon had performed badly against Russian forces in Ukraine. The company has tried to improve its drones based on the lessons from that war. But the U.S. military has reportedly cut back on buying them. Taiwan can afford a lot of weaponry. Its foreign-currency reserves, the sixth-largest in the world, are worth roughly $600 billion. But a defense bill had been stuck for months in Wang’s committee. Its opponents in the Nationalist Party had turned the budget debate into a proxy battle over Taiwan’s future, its relations with China, and its reliance on the United States. The need for a new budget arose late last year, when the Trump administration approved the biggest sale of American weapons to Taiwan in history. The $11 billion package included rocket launchers, artillery, anti-tank missiles, and other sophisticated gear. The following week, the Chinese military responded with a series of drills off the shores of Taiwan, firing missiles and rehearsing a blockade. Taiwan’s ruling party used the threats to argue for an additional package of American weapons, this one worth $14 billion. But President Lai needed Parliament to approve all the extra spending. In addition to the annual defense budget, about $20 billion, he sought a special disbursement of more than $40 billion over the next eight years. Roughly three-quarters of it would go toward American arms, while the rest would aid the development of Taiwan’s own defense industry, especially its drone factories. According to most polls, the extra spending bill had broad support. Voters saw it as a way to deter China and keep the Americans happy. It would satisfy Trump’s demand that all U.S. allies spend more on defense. It would also create jobs in the military industry and stimulate the broader economy, including in districts that the Nationalist Party controls. But the plan faced stiff resistance: In a phone call in February, Xi warned Trump to show “extreme caution.” The White House then decided to withhold the bulk of the arms packages until Xi and Trump had a chance to discuss them in person. Since their summit in May, the sales remain on hold. For Wang, arms deals double as a test of American resolve. No other country sells weapons to Taiwan. “So it’s a matter of life and death to Taiwan,” Wang said. He did not object to the U.S. pursuing dialogue with China, but he warned that Taiwan must not be used as some kind of bargaining chip. Would Trump treat it like one? Wang looked at his hands for a long moment. “I don’t know,” he said finally. “I don’t criticize our allies.” In the end, the legislators agreed to a pared-down budget worth about $25 billion. Practically all of it would go toward the purchase of American weapons—the Nationalists managed to cut the money for Taiwan’s drone industry. Nothing was left for the kinds of inexpensive, homegrown systems that Ukraine has used to fight the Russians. In late June, the Nationalists submitted a competing plan to meet the military’s need for drones. It included a lot more oversight and red tape that would slow the process down. Parliament has since been deadlocked over which plan to pursue, leaving the nascent drone program in limbo, which is precisely where China wants it. After her meeting with Xi in April, Cheng returned to Taiwan with a package of support from the Chinese Communist Party, called “the 10 incentives.” Their provisions would allow Taiwanese farmers, fishermen, and people in some other industries to sell food and goods in China. They also promised that certain TV shows, documentaries, and cartoons from Taiwan would be shown on the mainland (with proper vetting, of course). Chinese state media cast the incentives as a gift from Xi to the Nationalist Party. Friendly behavior, they suggested, would elicit additional favor. Taiwan’s government has tried to block the incentives, calling them a blatant form of political interference. At the very least, they offered a preview of how the Chinese might try to influence Taiwan’s next presidential election, which is due to take place in 2028. Cheng has suggested that she intends to run, and her campaign will no doubt benefit from the support of China, which she portrays as a generous neighbor. “They have become so modern,” she told me. During her visit to the mainland, officials in Shanghai treated her to an order of bubble tea—a Taiwanese invention—that was delivered via drone. The delegation also attended a performance of Mozart and Tchaikovsky at Beijing’s National Centre for the Performing Arts. “Their chief violinist was actually an American!” Cheng told me through an interpreter. “So I think China has become more confident. They’re more inclusive and more globalized.” When I asked what China might want in return for its friendly incentives, she seemed surprised at my suggestion that the relationship could come with strings attached. Xi had assured her, she said, that China would respect Taiwan’s system of government. He had not talked about making Taiwan a part of China. He had not brought up the experience of Hong Kong, whose democratic institutions survived for less than a decade after the British colony’s absorption into China in 1997. Wouldn’t China seek to do the same to Taiwan? “No,” she said, using the English word to make sure I understood. “We do not need to sacrifice or make any compromise regarding Taiwan’s freedom, democracy, economy, or our rule of law.” But China has already worked hard to curtail the rights of Taiwan and its elected leaders. Because of China’s decades-long pressure campaign, only 11 nations have formal relations with Taiwan. In April, China prevented President Lai from visiting one of them: At the request of Beijing, the Seychelles, Mauritius, and Madagascar refused to grant Lai’s plane the right to transit their airspace, forcing him to postpone his trip to Eswatini, a landlocked country in southern Africa. When I asked Cheng about the incident, she shrugged it off, saying Lai had brought it on himself “due to his own policies.” Last year, a few months after Trump began his second term, his administration denied Lai’s request to stop in the U.S. on his way to Latin America. But Cheng and the Nationalists have faced no restrictions. Less than two months after her visit to Beijing, Cheng led a delegation of party members on a trip to Washington and several other U.S. cities. During an appearance at the Asia Society, in Manhattan, she cast Xi as a reliable partner and repeated her assertion that China and Taiwan belong together. The moderators pressed her to explain how Taiwan’s democracy could survive such a partnership. “It’s a win-win scenario,” Cheng said, recalling how Xi had promised her that “anything can be negotiated.” In their seats near the front, several experts on Taiwan and retired U.S. diplomats exchanged skeptical looks and rolled their eyes. One whispered to me that Cheng sounded like “a whack job.” To me, she seemed more like an opportunist. At a time of lost trust in American resolve, she has reached for the bargain China is offering. Voters in Taiwan can feel the balance of power shifting, and many will choose the side that looks more stable, even at the risk of their own liberties. Near the end of Cheng’s talk in Manhattan, a member of the audience stood up and urged her not to pursue this deal. He warned that China would demolish Taiwan’s democracy, as it had demolished democratic processes in Hong Kong, and he demanded to know why Cheng had blocked funding for the drones that Taiwan needs. “Xi Jinping is a dictator!” the man shouted. Security guards then seized his microphone and removed him from the hall. Cheng seemed to enjoy the spectacle. “Drones should not be used as weapons,” she said with a smile, “but they should be used to deliver bubble tea.” Kinmen island, the westernmost outpost of sovereign Taiwanese territory, used to be a fortress. It sits only a few miles off the coast of China, close enough for a good swimmer to make it across to the mainland. In the late 1940s, the retreating Nationalists built bunkers here, positioned tanks, and studded the coastline with metal spikes to impale enemy landing craft. Thousands of Communist soldiers stormed these beaches during the Battle of Kinmen, in October 1949. Most were killed, the rest taken prisoner. Today, several monuments commemorate this part of Taiwan’s origin story, and a museum stands near the site of the battle. When I visited this spring, the guides urged me not to miss the immersive experience. It featured a dark room with eerie music and animations of war on the walls. Cameras took my photo and projected it onto a battle scene, inviting me to see myself as a defender of Kinmen. Only a few people alive in Taiwan today can remember fighting a war against China, and the simulation felt like an attempt to help younger generations imagine that experience. Many schoolchildren come to the museum on field trips. But the more common visitors lately are tourists from mainland China. Ferries bring them to Kinmen several times a day, and local businesses ply them with fried fish and sorghum liquor, the pungent local specialty. Such visits are part of China’s strategy. In a treatise published in 2023, the Central Committee of the Chinese Communist Party laid out a plan to pamper Kinmen with investment and tourism, turning the island into part of a “demonstration zone” to show what life might be like after the “complete reunification of the motherland.” The plan calls ultimately for a bridge to link Kinmen and the closest city on the mainland, Xiamen, about two miles away. At the start of his career, Xi Jinping served as the vice mayor of Xiamen. His preferred path for absorbing Taiwan into China has always relied on convincing the Taiwanese that life would improve under Beijing’s rule. China’s growth has made that easier. In the early 1990s, Taiwan’s economy was more than half the size of China’s. Now it’s one-20th the size. Residents of Kinmen can see the skyscrapers in Xiamen. They will soon be able to see jets landing at its new international airport. Many of them feel a greater kinship with the mainland than they do with Taipei. Atop one of the bluffs closest to China, a tower of speakers still broadcasts Taiwanese propaganda toward the mainland in a loop, calling on the Communists to defect and gain their liberty. The recording used to play loudly enough for people on the mainland to hear it. But local authorities have turned down the volume, converting the speakers into a tourist attraction. On the beach below, metal spikes still protrude from the sand, tilted at China, eroded by salt water and covered in barnacles. Wooden signs with skulls and crossbones used to warn about the risk of land mines on this coast. But the last of the mines have been cleared away, and the government has declared the beach open for swimming. On my last afternoon on the island, I decided to make my way toward China. My clumsy backstroke carried me 100 yards or so, just far enough to be able to look back at the coast and see that it no longer resembles anyone’s idea of a geopolitical flash point. There were no lookout posts or fortifications, no Taiwanese patrol boats. Only the battle museum and the tower of speakers, playing a message that no one in China can hear. This article appears in the October 2026 print edition with the headline “Will Taiwan Just Give Up?” When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.
