How the Government Wastes Our Time
2026-09-07

How the Government Wastes Our Time

The Atlantic | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Subscribe here: Apple Podcasts | Spotify | YouTube | Overcast | Pocket Casts Americans are bogged down in paperwork for reasons that are sometimes necessary, sometimes thoughtless—and, at times, nefarious. In her new book, The Time Tax: How the Government Wastes Our Time—And How to Fix It, my colleague Annie Lowrey looks at all those hours spent looking for tax documents or trying to certify income for the Free Application for Federal Student Aid, or the FAFSA and finds that things don’t have to be this way. A time tax is an infliction of “paperwork, effort, delay, and aggravation” that Americans suffer to get the rights they are guaranteed. We have the right to vote in a democracy; a time tax creates obstacles to those rights and makes realizing them cumbersome: They show up as extra identification requirements, when voter fraud is incredibly rare—or, in Jim Crow, as literacy tests. So many things are easy now. If I get hungry, I can order food from just about anywhere with just a few swipes of my thumb; one click at midnight, and diapers can be at the door before the sun rises. Why is there still such sludge in our bureaucratic systems? On this episode of Radio Atlantic, Annie joins me to discuss how these time taxes show up in our lives and waste our time, and how to make our government more efficient. The following is a transcript of the episode: Annie Lowrey: I think if instead of saying, This is about the poor people getting this program, or This is about making sure that these programs are secure, we could have a politics that says, This is malign; this is bad policy making. We give the government $8 trillion a year, and it passes back this garbage? No! No. Adam Harris: This is Radio Atlantic. I’m Adam Harris, and today we need to think about all of the ways that the government wastes our time. I’m only half-joking. If you’ve ever thought to yourself while filling out your taxes or trying to cancel a gym membership that This is taking too long! Don’t they already have this information? There has to be a better way!—you’re not alone. My colleague Annie Lowrey has a name for these cumbersome processes: She calls them “time taxes.” She wrote about the phenomenon for The Atlantic in 2021, and in her new book, The Time Tax: How the Government Wastes Our Time—And How to Fix It, she explores how and why our time is squandered when it does not have to be. So, what gets lost in the hours spent filling out paperwork? And how do we fix it? Annie joins me now to discuss. Annie, welcome to the show. Annie Lowrey: Thank you for having me, Adam. Harris: So, I wanna start by talking about the DMV. Harris: Because, right, it’s Labor Day, government offices are closed, but tomorrow across the country, DMVs are gonna be full of people trying to renew their car registrations, get Real IDs, change their addresses, change their names—whatever it might be. But those long lines at the DMV, I think, are the most trenchant example of what you call a “time tax.” So what are time taxes, and how do they show up in everyday lives, even outside of the DMV? Lowrey: So time taxes, these show up everywhere you are encountering the government, and really everywhere you’re interacting with businesses, right? It doesn’t necessarily have to be the government. And a time tax—or what’s called in the academic literature “an administrative burden”—is merely the person who is being bureaucratized, their experience of bureaucracy. So how much work they have to do to apply to something; how much work they have to do to research it; the fees that they might have to pay in some cases; and also their psychological or emotional experience, whether that’s DMV-level frustration or emotional anguish in a welfare program, or even a sense of civic pride that you might get when you’re interacting with a service that makes you feel good about yourself. Harris: So this is how people sort of interact with different things, right? So like you said, it’s not just the government—it can also be canceling a gym membership. In your new book, The Time Tax, you write about the idea that we see some of the most cumbersome examples of these time taxes as they relate to sort of social-safety-net programs. Food stamps, Medicaid, Social Security—things that people in need of help need to survive. So why is that? Why do we see a structure where the time taxes seem so cumbersome in those social-safety-net programs? Lowrey: We see time taxes as a general point, I think, being bigger, more onerous, more expensive in a broad sense of the word for programs that are affecting people with less social status, for whatever reason that might be—people with a history of being discriminated against, people viewed with some kind of skepticism by the state. So that could be people who weren’t born here, immigrants. That could be people with a history of interaction or involvement with the criminal-justice system. That could be different racial groups. White Americans are treated very differently than Black Americans. And absolutely, high-income Americans are treated very different than low-income Americans. And importantly, I don’t think that’s because low-income Americans are the recipients of this generosity that high-income Americans aren’t. So high-income Americans, they absolutely use programs like Social Security retirement benefits. They use tax programs like the 529 college-savings program or the Home Mortgage Interest Deduction. They benefit from the exclusion of employer-sponsored health benefits in some cases. But we don’t think of those as benefits, right? Those are tax policies—that’s not a benefit. It absolutely is. To the government, in accounting terms, it’s the same thing. It’s just that we’re not asking you to pee in a cup for it, which we do for a lot of benefits where you have to apply and reapply and come face-to-face with a social worker and perhaps fill out forms that might be a hundred pages long. And none of those policies interact with each other; they’re really cumbersome. And I think that’s because we want to dissuade low-income people from using these, because we believe that some share of them are applying fraudulently. And there’s this thought that, you know, Maybe if you’re low income, you need to get a little bit better at paperwork in your life. You need to straighten up and figure yourself out. And this is an imposition of that moral onto you. Harris: Yeah, and it’s interesting that you mentioned all the paperwork, and that’s even if they know that the programs exist to help them, right? Harris: And that doesn’t—as you write, that’s not by accident, that historically these sort of cumbersome processes have been used as a form of social control that is as old as data itself. So can you explain that idea a little bit? That these time taxes aren’t something that popped up in the middle of the 1950s and 1960s with an explosion of bureaucracy. Lowrey: Absolutely not. So a really clear example of these are the voting rules set out in Jim Crow. They are all hypothetically race-neutral. Or perhaps not all, but mostly, right? They are race-neutral. But, you know, a poll tax is gonna hit lower-income people, namely Black people, way harder than it’s gonna hit higher-income people. Property rules—if you’re a property owner, that isn’t gonna be applied equally. And something like a grandfather clause is instead going to allow white people, white men, to be able to vote, whereas Black men whose ancestors were enslaved are not going to be able to vote. And so even now, if we scoot forward, look at some of the rules in the SNAP program, which is food stamps. You can’t buy prepared food with your SNAP benefits. So the rotisserie chickens at Costco or at your grocery store? You cannot get those. Right now, there’s a lot of debate over ensuring that SNAP benefits only go to “healthy food.” So we’re saying, This can only be for nutrition as the state sees it, and we’re worried that you’re buying junk food and eating it. Never mind the fact, I don’t know, maybe somebody is a runner or a diabetic. Maybe they have reasons to access that. Maybe poor kids should be able to have birthday cakes—that seems fine to me. But that is why it’s a tool of social control. We’re making people know that we are skeptical of their spending habits and we’re attempting to control those spending habits through these little time taxes in the program. And I feel like once you see these, you see them everywhere. Again, I think it’s actually really good to compare it with the home-mortgage interest deduction that, like, if you have a mortgage, you get it. That’s it. They’re not like, Oh, are you living in a floodplain? Are you designing your home in this way? There isn’t that kind of paternalism in there, where there’s a lot of paternalism in a lot of other benefits. Harris: During COVID, there were so many programs that were intended to simply help people who were struggling, right? Additional tax benefits and things like that. A lot of those programs were very clearly designed so that they reached the most people with the greatest ease, and they worked. Like the children’s tax credit—things like that worked. Lowrey: I think often we have multiple goals in mind with a program. So take TANF, which is the cash-welfare program. Its stated goal, or its original goal, is to end child poverty. TANF as it is currently constructed has no effect on the child-poverty rate, which is remarkable to me. That’s in part because it’s a small program. It’s also because the goal of TANF is to control the behavior of the mostly mothers who are using it. There’s very strict work requirements. If you screw up in TANF, you get sanctioned. You are treated as a fraud risk. You are in trouble in some way. Which is not true—even with the IRS, if you screw your taxes up a bit, they’ll ask for the money or they’ll send you the money if you screwed up in the wrong way. But there isn’t this implication that this was a fraudulent act—unless you’re outright committing tax fraud; I’m just saying if you’ve miscalculated something. And so this is a way that we shrink public programs, where we’re saying, if we’re looking at the example of means-tested programs, the question is not only, Do you have certain characteristics? Do you fall below a certain income line, taking into account the size of your family and some other things? It’s saying, Okay, but we also need you to be good enough at performing for the state, at filling out this paperwork, meeting with this counselor at this given time. And it’s just, you know, I don’t know that that’s a really legitimate way to go about it, as opposed to simply saying, Yeah, if you meet the income target, you should have this. Harris: When we come back we talk with Annie about how to address the time tax. Harris: So there were about 4 million people in the U.S. when the Constitution was signed in 1787. There are about 350 million people in the country now. And so, as the nation has grown, so has its bureaucracy and the systems that it uses. And over time, those systems age. It makes sense to want to update them, to want to make them better as technology improves. And at the beginning of the second Trump administration, the president established DOGE, the Department of Government Efficiency; appointed Elon Musk to head it up; charged it with cleaning up the government, modernizing old systems, and making it more efficient. You were working on this book when that began. What did you think of that effort at the time? Lowrey: A few things. So one was that this was presented as a novel thing. And no, no—there’s, like, more than a hundred years of presidents attempting to do this, saying, The systems have gotten too complicated; we’re gonna clean things up; we’re gonna make the entire system more efficient. So it wasn’t new. I think that the fundamental idea was a good one. I think that the way that they went about it was, like, I would call it clown college if it weren’t so destructive and cruel. They were just firing civil servants at random. They made all of the federal civil servants send a five-point email explaining what they had done every week. Lowrey: Yeah, exactly—wasting their time. There was no effort to look at the voluminous reports that we have from inspectors general, for instance, or the government’s various accountability offices to say, Okay, can we actually improve procurement contracts? Or, Could we find out the places where we’re really overspending on something, or, you know, We need some deep improvement. There was none of that. They hurt service delivery by getting rid of some critical people. They created all kinds of busywork. Not just the [five-point] emails. They slowed things down such that some agencies couldn’t get essential components that they would have needed without the approval of a political operative. So for instance, NASA had to get approval for, like, bolts. I’m pretty sure that NASA knows how to buy bolts, right? That is not the problem with an inefficiency in government. And they never considered the citizen experience. Do we really need to wait this long to vote? Do we have to do our own taxes? We don’t, by the way—the IRS would like to do them for us. And so it was just this all-out assault on the civil service. And it was the second time that we’ve seen it from President Trump, where he just believes that civil servants are a malign politicized force, that they’re there to take sinecures and draw money away from the taxpayer, when many of them, they just love their work—and they’re actually earning less than they would in private industry—because they’re really dedicated to their jobs and they’re very specialized at what they do. So, yes, DOGE—good idea. And I hope that that idea carries forward, but I hope that they do it much more effectively and efficiently going around the next time. I’d also note that the great sin of DOGE, too, is that it might end up costing the government money because it caused so many lawsuits. They did so many things without the appropriate permissions, without the legal changes that they needed, that they’ve just been mired in lawsuits. Harris: So when you say we don’t have to do our taxes. The IRS would like to do our taxes for us? What do you mean by that? Lowrey: Yes, you have to pay your taxes; you have to do your taxes. What I mean is that nine in 10 American households take the standard deduction, which means that they’re not itemizing their taxes. Generally, it’s because their tax situation is pretty straightforward. And so the IRS has all of the information it needs already to do your taxes for you. They have your payroll data from your employer. If you have a retirement account, they’ll have those financial records from the institution, from your bank or your lender. They’ll have local data as well. So they could merely pre-fill your 1040 for you and send it to you to look over, or they could just send you a summary and say, Okay, if you agree with this, just let us know. You don’t really have to do anything. We’ll send you your refund, or there won’t be any money exchanging hands at all. And so this is the way that taxes work in a lot of our peer countries with high incomes. In Japan, if you’re earning a salary, for the most part you don’t have to do anything. In some countries in Europe, you can do it all by text message. And the IRS is legally prevented from doing this, such that the average American spends 12 hours a year on the 1040 and spends roughly $300 on tax-preparation services. It’s silly. It’s silly. And there’s some people with very complicated tax situations. Fine. The IRS might have some requests for information. Did you have another kid this year? Did you take out another mortgage? Something like that. But they don’t need these endless, endless, endless, endless forms. They don’t need you to do them or hire somebody else to do them. They could do it. Harris: To that point, are there other ways that we could be doing things? Is there a better way outside of these time taxes? How can the U.S. make government programs more accessible? Lowrey: There’s a million and one. And it’s almost—there’s so many options out there that I feel like sometimes it’s hard for me to summarize. So one straightforward thing is that there are different income standards and eligibility standards that target the same group of people using the same programs, many of whom use multiple programs. So for instance, Medicaid and SNAP—they have different income standards. In some cases, they have different standards of qualifying income, and you can’t cross enroll. So you can’t say, Okay, if I’m eligible for this, then I’ll maybe also be eligible for that. Sometimes that’s because of a difficult reason, so consider jobless insurance, which people often use if they’ve lost their job. That goes to the individual, where SNAP is really aimed at households instead. So that’s actually kind of a technical thing. But just unifying these standards and making such that you could cross-enroll in programs would be a wonderful, wonderful thing to do. Holding states and offices and DMVs and everything else accountable for counting how much paperwork they’re putting on people, how many people they are not serving or are churning off and on programs because the paperwork is too much, and then saying, Okay, we want you to bring these numbers down—that would be really helpful. We don’t do that. In terms of health care, dealing with hospitals and insurers and doctors is absolute misery. There’s a lot of common-sense regulation that could prevent health-care providers and insurers from denying procedures that should have been approved, or making sure that your medical bill is actually accurate—which half of them aren’t—preventing them from providing you with what you are paying for through simple paperwork and attrition and frustration, as opposed to some other legitimate tool. So those are just some ideas, but there are a lot out there. Harris: Well, I guess if there are so many ideas out there, why hasn’t the government used some of those ideas, utilized the modern technology in the same way that, if I wanted a burger right now, I could pull my phone forward and just order it from DoorDash and it’s here in 15 minutes. If I needed a car to get somewhere, it’s, like, two clicks and I’ve got it. We have all of this modern technology. Why haven’t they used that modern technology to streamline a lot of this stuff? Lowrey: Because the government is not really very good at reforming itself. A lot of these changes would have to be made by Congress, and Congress doesn’t really care; it doesn’t see itself as a benefits administrator. And we’ve screwed up the lines of responsibility here. So if you’re not getting a benefit that you were supposed to be receiving, who’s at fault? Is that the state administrator? Is that the office that you’re working with, where if you complain, you are concerned that you might be retaliated against? Is that the governor? Is that the president? Because he’s the administrator of the programs, right? Is that Congress? And I also think that there’s these broken feedback loops, by which I mean: If you get Social Security retirement benefits, we have decades of beautiful political-science research showing that it makes you feel like a citizen; it makes you feel like a good person; it makes you more likely to vote; it makes you more likely to be civically engaged in lots of ways. And seniors, when there are threats to Social Security, they come out and they demand that politicians stop it. But there’s so many other little, tiny problems that relate to other programs that I don’t think that we have a mass politics demanding this. And so that’s why I think it’s hard. Nevertheless, when politicians do this stuff and make things easier, I think often it goes really well. It’s been really popular for, for instance, Zohran Mamdani in New York City. And I think that to a certain extent, we’re still operating under this understanding that these administrative burdens are demanded by the broad public because they’re so afraid of fraud in these programs. But there’s not that much fraud in these programs. We have really good ways to detect it. Giving more money to the civil service such that they could stamp fraud out would be a way to do it. And I just, I don’t know that people are voting on the basis of the way that the IRS is administering audits to other taxpayers. I don’t think that they are. Lowrey: Some of it. Everybody’s voting for something. Harris: About the sort of weaponization of data that we’ve seen over the last year and a half, and the counterargument to The government should do more things to make lives better with our data is that the government can also take our data and do things that are nefarious if they so chose to, right? You can look at the Stasi; you can look at the Pinochet regimes. How do you balance those concerns? Or does the government just already have so much data that they don’t necessarily factor in? Lowrey: I think it’s a real concern and a real threat, since we are in an environment of democratic backsliding. This is one of the reasons that mixed-status families with citizen children and perhaps undocumented parents don’t get safety-net benefits that they have every right to get. That kid is a citizen; they’re allowed to get it. It’s because they don’t want to invite this state that could be very threatening to them into their lives. I think one way to think about this is to give Americans the choice and to give the government permission: Okay, if I want this benefit, I feel comfortable with this office getting my data from the IRS. Something like that, so that it is elective and so that people can decide. Harris: You point to Estonia in the book as a sort of prime example of a place that’s successfully easing the administrative burden on their citizens. But you also mentioned that they’re a small country. And as you were talking about, the gargantuan task of making the systems talk to each other in the United States, it makes me think that we have a scale problem here, that we might just be too big to eliminate some of the cumbersome bureaucracy. But how do you square the Estonian example with our context in the U.S.? Lowrey: Yeah, Estonia is something we can aspire to, but I don’t think it’s ever gonna be something that we’re gonna have. So the thing about Estonia is they build a completely new government after they break away from the Soviet Union and regain their independence. So Estonia regains its independence in the summer of 1991, and they form a government completely de novo, from scratch, at that point. And they do so during a climate where they want Estonians to trust the government because so many didn’t due to the Soviet influence. And so they create the most elegant system of benefits that are all controlled by an ID number on all of these databases that are designed and updated constantly to talk to each other, such that Estonia has this policy that if you tell the government, any part of the government, a piece of information about yourself once, everything updates. So for instance, if you tell the voting authority, My address has changed, all of the hospitals know it. All the schools, everything knows it. All of your mail goes to your new address. It’s nothing like that in the United States at all. So I don’t think that we are gonna become that—again, a very small homogenous country that started in the 1990s functionally. But I think that we can become more Estonia-like. And they’ve also had some issues with data security. They take it really seriously. So maybe there’s some lessons from Estonia to us, too, about setting these systems up in a way that is secure. Harris: What would it take to implement some of those ideas from there? Is this just a political-will question? Lowrey: Yeah, I do think it is a political-will question. And I think it’s a political-framing question. Because very often, administrative burdens are framed as necessary to prevent fraud. They’re not terribly good at preventing fraud. They’re much better at preventing people from getting needed benefits. But I think if instead of saying, This is about the poor people getting this program, or this is about making sure that these programs are secure, we could have a politics that says, This is malign; this is bad policy making. We give the government $8 trillion a year, and it passes back this garbage? No! No. Right? At least do the obvious stuff here. And I also think that there’s ways of thinking about new policy changes that could be administratively light. So for instance, there’s always been this idea to put children up to the age of 18 on Medicare, which comes and goes and comes and goes. That’s a nice thing to do because it would mean that just kids are on Medicare. You never have to worry if your kid gets a broken bone or, God forbid, a cancer, that they’re always gonna be able to get treatment. But more than that, it’s just elegant. It’s simple. It’s the government taking work away from you. I think about this a lot in terms of free school meals as well. That’s not only a benefit for lower-income families that might be having nutritional challenges. It’s also like, If you don’t have food in the house just because you forgot, oh great, the school’s just gonna feed your kid and you don’t have to worry about it. It’s just a nice thing in terms of reducing those burdens. And so I think that a lot of that could be really popular. One thing I think about a lot is: We focus so much on reducing unnecessary burdens on businesses. Can we think about it in terms of reducing unnecessary burdens on families? Whether that’s these insurers that are not really insuring us, or this tax authority that makes us do our own taxes, or any other program where it’s just hard to grapple with. Harris: Annie, here is to hoping that that burden is alleviated. Thank you so much for joining. Lowrey: Thank you. I do hope. Thank you so much, Adam. Harris: That’s all for today’s show. The Last Thing Teenagers Will Let You Teach Them
2026-09-07

The Last Thing Teenagers Will Let You Teach Them

The Atlantic | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
A strange thing happened to me in a deserted parking lot on a recent Sunday morning: My 16-year-old daughter actually asked me a question. I was telling her everything I know about reversing a car without the help of a backup camera. “First turn your whole body, shoulders rotated, right hand on the back of the passenger seat, until you’re looking out the rear window at the world behind you.” Her hands were on the wheel. Her phone was face down in the cup holder. She turned her body the way I’d suggested and nudged the wheel to the left, and the car floated backward in a woozy arc that wasn’t quite what she’d intended. “Wait,” she said. “Do I turn with both hands? Did I do that wrong?” I want to stop here, because I don’t think I can convey just how unusual such an exchange is these days—her listening to me and asking for help. Sometime in the past year, she stopped wanting anything from me other than my credit card and maybe my nicest suede boots. She definitely stopped wanting my instruction, my opinion, my take on anything, having concluded that she already understands pretty much everything. The talking is what I have missed. For 14 or 15 years, my daughter narrated in a fire hose of chatter—to me, to her father, to whoever. So much talking: about why the sun sets, the kid she was mad at, the plot of a show she was three episodes into, how she wanted to decorate her room. Around the start of high school, the talking thinned out, siphoned almost exclusively to her friends. Nothing announced the change. It went the way a baby tooth goes, loose for a while and then simply absent. When she got a learner’s permit, some measure of talking reappeared. It wasn’t fully restored, exactly, but confined within one small, sealed space that moves. Inside the car, she knows she doesn’t know: In no version of parallel parking can she wing it without asking for tips from someone who has done it 10,000 times. Learning to drive is something she cannot fake her way through. This wobbly uncertainty, this return to a kind of innocence, is the rarest condition in a 16-year-old, and it lasts about as long as a learner’s permit does. So my daughter asks, and listens, and we manage to simply talk in some of the ways we haven’t in a long time: about what her friends did over the summer, which kid at the pool where she lifeguards asked her if she could babysit them. For an hour at a stretch I get a version of my daughter that I had filed away as gone for good. But this is also, in some ways, the worst hour of my week, because the terms of a driving lesson are the exact inverse of the many other kinds of lessons that came before it. Everything else I ever taught my daughter, I did with one hand on the thing: I steadied the tail end of the bike, I held the knife and moved her fingers back from the blade, I grasped her hand crossing Seventh Avenue for so many years that my palm still expects to find hers at intersections. In the car, though, she has the wheel, and I have the fear. My only instrument is my voice, which is not connected to anything that stops the car. Parents say they’re letting go or loosening the reins or handing over the keys, as if adolescence were a series of small, deliberate releases, each one manageable. Then you hand over the actual keys, and the metaphor becomes a physical arrangement staged on a public road at 40 miles an hour, at which point it breaks. First there is supervised driving, and soon there is nothing. In between, the passenger seat is one of the few places on Earth where a parent gets to occupy two conditions at once: maximum proximity plus zero control. I have thought about this a lot from that seat, just 18 inches from my daughter, slowly understanding that being nearby was never the same as keeping her safe. Ever since she was born, I had assumed that being close to her was a form of protecting her. I could catch her if she slipped. In the car, that assumption comes apart, and all I can do is sit there with my fear. What surprised me is that the fear runs both ways. For the first time in years, my daughter is frightened of something in front of both of us rather than scared of something I might do to embarrass her—that is, afraid of me. That distinction turns out to be enormous. Since she hit adolescence, her anxiety, in my presence, has generally been about whether I would say something wacky (yes!), make a dumb joke (also yes!), or exist too loudly in a space containing her peers (duh). I have been the weather she is bracing against. But in the car, we are braced against the same thing, and it is outside the windshield. A massive truck backs out without looking; a cyclist suddenly appears. We both suck in air at the same instant, and her hand and my hand tighten—hers on the wheel, mine on nothing. Coming to a stop at a red light, she sees me sending a text from the passenger seat. “I can’t understand how anyone texts and drives,” she says, almost bewildered, the way a fifth grader might talk about someone doing calculus in their head. At that moment, she is using every part of her brain to keep a two-ton object between two painted lines, and the idea that a person could do this and tap out a message strikes her as physically impossible. I agree with her, but what I don’t say is that practicing will help her rethink this. Competence makes the risk available. One day she will be good enough at driving to consider being more careless about it. As she improves and gains confidence, the driving, for her, slowly grows less frightening and more thrilling. She presses the accelerator, her foot more sure, and we both know that she is on the edge of a huge new expanse of freedom and change—that driving lessons are not about a test to pass but a way to get farther than she has ever gotten on her own. The shared fear is temporary, and I can already feel the expiration date on it. Some Sunday soon, we’ll have our last lesson. And what I’ll miss isn’t the driving; it’s the asking—the tacit admission that she doesn’t know everything—and how she lets her guard down while she’s admitting it. When the questions end, she’ll be very much on her way, and I’ll be the boots and the credit card again, which, I remind myself, is part of the assignment too.

The DSA’s Problem Isn’t ‘Media Training’
2026-09-07

The DSA’s Problem Isn’t ‘Media Training’

The Atlantic | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
American socialists are convinced that they have a media-training problem. In the past year, members of the Democratic Socialists of America, the country’s largest leftist organization, have won electoral victories in places such as New York City and Michigan, drawing a spotlight that the group has struggled to handle. Last month, the DSA’s co-chairs, Megan Romer and Ashik Siddique, gave several high-profile interviews that even many of their most fervent supporters agree were disastrous. The two leaders struggled to give straight answers to simple questions, and they defended outlandish policies, such as abolishing prisons and police. They seemed to present socialists exactly the way their opponents caricature them: as extremist, unserious, and unprepared. In damage-control mode, many of DSA’s defenders on social media are repeating the phrase media training as though it were a mantra. If only the group’s leaders had been better prepared to deal with media outlets, the thinking goes, they would have avoided this train wreck. The DSA’s communications director, Priscilla Yeverino, recently told Axios, “Our leaders aren’t politicians. They’re working-class people and new to doing media.” The idea that the DSA’s troubles on prime-time television are a technical matter solvable by the right training is surely comforting, but it is also wrong. The group has a bigger problem: It is led by unpractical extremists whose views are a far cry from those that its electorally successful figures, such as Representative Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez and New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani, espouse. This disconnect is partly explained by a sharp division within the DSA. One wing engages in mass politics and includes AOC, Mamdani, and other socialists committed to democratic principles. The other wing is sectarian and extremist: It evinces little commitment to democracy and includes open admirers of Hamas, the Iranian regime, and historical dictators such as Mao Zedong. If the interviews with the DSA’s co-chairs were embarrassing, that’s a result not of a lackluster media performance but of the actual positions the group has embraced. A closer look at the interviews confirms this. Romer, who belongs to the sectarian wing, evidently did not know what the 2000 Camp David Summit was and could not say what “taxing the hell out of millionaires,” as she demanded, would look like in practice. When asked about authoritarianism in Cuba, she praised the country’s constitution and suggested that Americans should learn from it. Maybe media training could have taught Romer what happened at Camp David and what a tax policy might entail. It still wouldn’t have made the pro-authoritarian politics she shares with her faction of the DSA more palatable. In December, Romer’s Red Star caucus, a self-declared Marxist-Leninist faction of the DSA, posted quotes from a speech by Mao in “celebration” of the Chinese Communist leader’s 132nd birthday. Members of the faction study the works of Mao and the Soviet dictator Joseph Stalin as part of their political education. Red Star has declared its unequivocal sympathy for Hamas and praised the “good judgement” of “our socialist siblings” in Palestine for working with the Islamist organization and “external groups like Iran, Hezbollah, and Ansarallah under a principle they call unity of the fields.” In other words, Red Star is an admirer of Iran’s notorious “Axis of Resistance.” That Romer wouldn’t critique authoritarianism in Cuba is not a lapse but an expression of her faction’s politics. Red Star’s stated policy couldn’t be any clearer about this: “To publicly critique a government or movement that stands in opposition to the American government is to do the U.S. State Department’s work for them, from Iran to Palestine.” In other words, any state or group that opposes the American government should be beyond criticism. Liberation, the DSA’s “Marxist-Leninist-Maoist caucus,” likewise declares the United States to be “not a nation” but a “prison-house of nations, with the Euro-Americans being the jailer.” Unlike Romer, Siddique belongs to the mainstream wing of the DSA, but he is bound by the group’s statements and broader culture. The sectarian wing currently dominates the DSA: The group’s convention in Chicago last year rejected a resolution opposing “all governments that engage in the repression of democratic rights.” Thus, when asked repeatedly what he thought of Fidel Castro, whom the DSA had praised in an earlier statement, Siddique awkwardly referred to “inequality in the United States” and criticized U.S. policy—deflecting the question and offering sound bites instead of engagement, much as an establishment pol might do. David Brooks: Democrats became great by fighting the left This pro-left-authoritarian orientation explains why DSA electoral observers shamefully reported no fraud when they were dispatched to monitor the electoral sham organized by Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro in 2024. Those results were so bogus that even the left-wing governments of Brazil, Chile, and Uruguay couldn’t accept them. Yet at a DSA session on the subject, a pro-Maduro organization’s assessment that Venezuela’s electoral system was “one of the very best in the world” was presented as fact, as was the false claim that Venezuela’s main opposition candidates were “fascist.” The two wings of the DSA are committed to wildly different methods. Mamdani governs New York City partly as a technocrat, and AOC has sought to influence the Democratic Party from within. One of the DSA’s sectarian factions, by contrast, recently suggested that socialist lawmakers “should do everything in their power to disrupt any Congressional business that does not benefit the working class or advance the socialist program.” They should, it went on, “creatively abuse their powers to make it difficult to conduct legislative business,” for instance by packing the galleries of Congress with hecklers. This faction seems to view elected lawmakers as “tribunes” for the socialist movement rather than as people who, you know, make laws. The sectarian wing got the DSA to drop its endorsement of AOC and refuse to support Kamala Harris in the 2024 presidential election. Red Star argued against endorsing AOC unless she withdrew her support for Joe Biden (then the Democratic nominee), and it refused to fundraise for the Democratic leadership or central congressional campaign. But ultimately, the DSA dropped AOC on the grounds that she was not sufficiently anti-Israel—a singular obsession of the sectarian wing. (The foreign-policy section of the DSA’s political platform discusses the details of only one country’s politics, and that’s Israel and Palestine, in which it calls for the self-determination of Palestinians without making a similar provision for Israeli Jews.) For all of the extremist wing’s influence, however, my years of research into the group suggest that the DSA’s members are mostly not members of any caucuses. The average DSA member probably has more in common with AOC than with Mao or Hamas, and the nonsectarians seem especially dominant in the group’s large urban chapters—such as in New York City, where the DSA has more than 15,000 of its approximately 120,000 members. More than 80 percent of DSA members in the Bronx and Queens voted in 2024 to endorse AOC. The sectarian wing came to control much of the organization’s leadership by manipulating internal processes, which is one reason it opposes organizational referenda and other one-member-one-vote procedures. The DSA’s mainstream wing includes thinkers who are committed to democracy, critical of authoritarianism (including that of socialist regimes, past and present), and who reject some of the signature policies of the sectarian wing, such as police abolition. These figures have been vocal in response to last month’s media fiasco. Eric Blanc, a labor historian at Rutgers, sharply criticized Romer for “falling into a trap being set by the establishment,” which seeks “to paint DSA as extreme.” Like others in his wing, Blanc advocates for adopting a Nordic-style social democracy as a step toward overcoming capitalism. This position is also shared by the Jacobin founder and former DSA Vice Chair Bhaskar Sunkara, whose 2019 Socialist Manifesto explores Swedish social democracy in envisioning a socialism for the 21st century. Reacting to the DSA’s recent media debacles, Ben Burgis, a philosopher and Jacobin writer, opined: “We don’t do ourselves any favors by refusing to say anything too negative about left-wing dictatorships targeted by US foreign policy,” especially “if we’re trying to argue that the failures of authoritarian state socialism in the twentieth century shouldn’t mean that capitalism is the best humanity will ever be able to do.” The group has failed to reassure its critics of its commitment to multiparty democracy, Burgis added. Thinkers in this wing of the DSA take seriously the imperative to reconcile socialist commitments to economic justice with democratic and liberal values. Burgis, Sunkara, and the economist Mike Beggs have co-authored a forthcoming book called The Blueprint: How Socialism Can Work in the Real World, which attempts to offer a “realistic model” of a post-capitalist economic system. Drawing on the work of the liberal philosopher John Rawls, as well as detailed economic research, the book takes the Soviet Union to task for a “record of systematic economic failure, not just political repression” and suggests a model that mixes social ownership with a significant role for the market. It also engages the arguments of well-known critics of socialism, such as Friedrich Hayek and Ludwig von Mises. This work puts the flippancy of Romer’s “taxing the hell out of millionaires” comment to shame. But if the likes of AOC, Sunkara, Burgis, and Blanc wish for the American public to give their notions of an economically rigorous and democratically committed socialism a chance, they need to break with the fans of Mao and Hamas. So long as they share an organization with the DSA’s sectarians, they can’t complain when the media associates the DSA—and American socialism—with the extreme positions that it formally adopts and that its leaders espouse. No amount of media training can solve that.

Induced demand does not mean that building roads never reduces congestion; new capacity can provide substantial and sometimes lasting benefits. Rather, it means that traffic responds to changes in the cost and availability of road travel, just as traffic can decrease when road capacity is reduced through people changing routes, times, destinations, modes, or simply making fewer trips. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Trump Has Brutalized Blue States in His Second Term. There’s a Way for Democrats to Fight Back.
2026-09-08

Trump Has Brutalized Blue States in His Second Term. There’s a Way for Democrats to Fight Back.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
This is the second in a series of articles, “Try, Try Again?,” drawn from my new book—Great and Dangerous Offences: The Case For (and Against) Impeaching Trump a Third Time—due out in early 2027. The Constitution created a federal republic consisting of states with equal legal status. Under the 10th Amendment, Congress and the executive branch have only limited authority to intrude on the sovereign prerogatives of the states. Individual residents of those states are guaranteed equal protection of the laws. These are pillars of the American constitutional order. To subvert any of them is to subvert the Constitution itself, a subversion that, as the Framers insisted, is impeachable. If a president engages in a pattern of conduct that subordinates the rights and interests of individual states to his personal or partisan political interests, that would be impeachable. Likewise, if a president engages in a pattern of conduct that denies equal protection of the laws of the United States to the citizens of individual states in order to promote his personal or partisan political interests, that, too, would be impeachable. In his second term as president, Donald Trump has continuously done both. To understand how Trump has abused his power against the states, it’s important to understand the power the federal government—and the president, specifically—does have. For example, the widely varying geographic, demographic, social, and economic circumstances of the states has meant that they need different things from the central government. Thus, the unruly interaction of democratic politics with national government accepts a good deal of horse trading over which states will get various types of federal largesse and regulation, along with a degree of more or less constant disagreement between state and federal authorities over policy and resource allocation. In extreme cases, the federal government may employ coercion against states that are sufficiently out of harmony with national law and policy. If a state government openly violates federal law despite clear orders from the federal judiciary—as was the case of some Southern states during the civil rights conflicts of the 20th century—the national government may use appropriate measures, including force, to compel compliance with the law. This is not the current circumstance. Critically, the federal government has to enforce such measures itself rather than commandeering the states to do so. Congress lacks “the power to issue orders directly to the States.” It cannot compel states to issue regulations. It cannot command state officers to participate in or enforce federal laws under what is known as the anti-commandeering doctrine. While Congress can condition a state’s receipt of federal funds on compliance with specified conditions, there are limitations to this power. Congress’ conditions to states must be clear and unambiguous and must have some relationship to the purposes of the federal grants in question. The conditions cannot be imposed without a state’s knowing and voluntary consent. This means that Congress may not set conditions on dispersal of federal funds that effectively offer the state no realistic choice except compliance. While Congress has some authority to induce states to participate in federal initiatives, the president acting on his own authority has no such power. To be sure, the president and the executive branch are charged with lawfully implementing congressional directives about the dispersal of appropriated funds. But the president cannot discriminate between states in dispersing federal funds by imposing his own rules, policy stipulations, or personal preferences, particularly if they contradict or are unrelated to congressional funding directives. Moreover, the post–Civil War 14th Amendment made explicit that the individual inhabitants of states are entitled to equal treatment under national law, which means that people living in different states cannot be treated differently simply because one lives in a state politically aligned with the president and another does not. Trump has flagrantly violated these principles on multiple occasions during his second term. The first violation began on his first day back in office when he issued an executive order commanding that “sanctuary jurisdictions,” meaning states, counties, or municipalities that did not order their law-enforcement authorities to cooperate fully with Trump’s immigration-enforcement actions, “do not receive access to Federal funds.” The initial executive order was followed by two others with the same thrust. These orders are not based on any congressional enactment conditioning federal funding on state or local cooperation with federal immigration efforts. Trump renewed the threat to all federal funding in a statement in January 2026. Following Trump’s executive orders, various elements of the executive branch began to withhold funding to state, regional, and local governments deemed insufficiently compliant with, or openly resistant to, Trump’s immigration program. A number of cities and states that suffered funding cuts sued. At least one court has ruled the cuts illegal. Multiple cases are pending. It is relevant to any article of impeachment premised wholly or in part on coercion of the states over immigration policy that Trump tried essentially the same tactic in his first administration and was blocked by the federal courts. In short, the courts previously ruled that Trump impinged on the sovereignty of the states and ignored Congress’ power of the purse, but he came right back and did it again. This sort of repeat-offender behavior elevates what might otherwise be considered a simple policy or legal dispute into an impeachable offense. As troublesome as Trump’s extortion of state cooperation with his immigration efforts may be, it is at least connected to an articulable policy objective, however controversial. Two other types of denial of federal funds, grants, or programs are even more concerning, and even more compelling as grounds for impeachment. First, the Trump administration has actively discriminated against “blue” states by systemically withholding federal funding, grants, and programmatic assistance. The discrimination is self-evident from the available data. A review of presidential disaster declarations entitling states and localities to federal aid revealed that in Trump’s first 14 months in office, he approved a mere 23 percent of applications from states with a Democratic governor and two Democratic senators, while approving 89 percent of requests from states with a Republican governor and two Republican senators. Moreover, approvals of disaster declarations in Democratic states took twice as long as those to Republican states (80 days vs. 40 days). No pattern remotely similar has ever been observed for past presidents. Moreover, one need not rely on inferences from data. In the fall of 2025, Russell Vought, head of the Office of Management and Budget, announced cancellation of “nearly $8 billion” in grants for clean-energy programs authorized by Congress during the Biden administration—what Vought called the “Green New Scam.” The first indication that these cancellations, in addition to being open violations of Congress’ power of the purse, were based primarily on politics rather than climate policy was that he listed the states in which grants were canceled—and they included no states dominated by Republicans and instead included 16 of the 19 states that had voted for Kamala Harris in the 2024 election. Multiple states, a coalition including energy and environmental organizations and the city of St. Paul, Minnesota, a group of University of California researchers, and others sued the Trump administration over the cancellations. In pleadings filed in the researchers’ case, the U.S. Department of Energy formally stipulated that none of the 284 grants terminated in October 2025 were terminated “based on any programmatic, statutory, cost-reduction or performance-based factor.” The stipulation admitted that, “with one exception, the 284 terminated grants had a recipient location and/or at least one place of performance in a state that awarded its electoral votes to Kamala Harris in the 2024 election and has two Democratic-caucusing senators.” The stipulation concluded by confessing: DOE accepts that the inclusion of grants in the October notice tranche was based solely on the political identity of the grant recipient’s state. … DOE accepts that the differential treatment resulting in the October 2025 termination of Blue State grants and the non-termination of non-Blue State grants was not based on a rational connection between the recipient’s location and/or place of performance and DOE’s past or current agency priorities. As reported by the New York Times, at the same time as the administration was cancelling grants to “blue states,” it “left untouched hundreds of additional energy grants in states that were represented by Republicans and had backed Trump in the last election.” In short, the Trump administration has admitted using payment or withholding of congressionally appropriated funds to punish or retaliate against states and their inhabitants because, and only because, those states support the president’s political opposition. Second, Trump has gone beyond generic discrimination against states controlled by Democrats and, in at least one case, organized a multipronged attack on a single state because its criminal justice system prosecuted, convicted, and refused to release a Trump backer and election denier. Following the 2020 election, Tina Peters, the clerk and recorder of Mesa County, Colorado, was convicted of multiple felonies for breaching the county election systems in a supposed effort to prove that the election had been rigged and lying about it thereafter. In October 2024, Peters was sentenced to nine years in prison. In the years since 2020, Peters had become a prominent figure in the election-denialist circles supporting Trump, persisting in unsubstantiated claims that somehow Trump had been cheated out of the election against Joe Biden. When Peters was sentenced, Trump declared himself outraged. In August 2025, he demanded that Colorado release Peters, threatening that if it did not, “I am going to take harsh measures.” On Dec. 11, 2025, Trump issued what he characterized as a pardon of Peters. The president’s pardon power does not extend to state crimes. At the same time Trump was issuing his counterfeit pardon, he was reportedly fuming behind the scenes. On Dec. 15, 2025, White House special assistant Emily Underwood sent an email to five federal agencies organizing an urgent “brainstorm call” in order to, in her words, “prioritize Colorado for the purposes of ensuring grants and federal support are in line with administration priorities.” The next day, the Departments of Transportation and Energy, which had been invited to the “brainstorm,” canceled hundreds of millions of dollars in federal grants for projects in Colorado. In addition to the cuts in transportation and energy projects, two days after the “brainstorm” call, the Trump administration ordered that Boulder’s National Center for Atmospheric Research, a world leader in climate and earth systems research, be dismantled, shuttering some programs and moving others out of state. In late December, Trump denied two Colorado disaster relief requests stemming from wildfires and a flood. Also in December, the Department of Agriculture attacked Colorado’s federally supported Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program by imposing new requirements that would have been impossible to meet. On Dec. 30, 2025, Trump used his presidential veto to kill a congressionally authorized water project for the Eastern Plains region of Colorado. If there were any doubt about the connection between all these actions and Trump’s fury over the Peters prosecution, Trump removed them with a New Year’s Eve social media post in which he said, of Colorado Gov. Jared Polis and the Republican district attorney who prosecuted Peters: “I wish them only the worst,” and they should “rot in Hell.” Trump’s assault on Colorado was, if anything, more constitutionally egregious than his general attacks on other Democratic states. In his actions against other states, Trump and his minions were simply giving rein to general political animus or, in some cases, selectively applying his peculiar policy fixations, such as his hatred of clean energy. In Colorado, Trump employed the might of the federal government to coerce a state into granting undeserved personal relief to one properly convicted state felon. Worse, the felon in question was a figurehead in Trump’s ongoing effort to destroy confidence in the nation’s electoral system. Worst of all, the pressure worked: Polis granted Peters clemency in May and she was released in June, after having served less than a quarter of her original sentence. This was as blatant, and impeachable, an abuse of presidential power as can well be imagined. Multiple courts have entered orders blocking parts of Trump’s funding and program cancellations in states led by Democrats. In particular, in January 2026, a federal judge enjoined the assault on Colorado’s SNAP requirements. The move to dismantle NCAR was enjoined by the same federal court in June 2026; the court determined that the government’s action was procedurally “arbitrary and capricious” and that “retaliation played at least some role.” However, the question of whether presidential action is impeachable does not turn on the judgments of courts, particularly when the essential ground for an article of impeachment is the president’s active subversion of the basic constitutional design. It is absolutely plain that the president may not consciously discriminate against a state government or the residents of a state based purely on the partisan political alignment of the state’s residents or its government. Nor can a president enlist the power of the federal government to coerce a state’s justice system into granting special treatment to a personal favorite of the president, particularly if that favorite’s crime was committed in aid of the president’s own attack on the nation’s electoral system. Considered together, Trump’s partisan attacks on Democratic states and their residents amount to an abuse of presidential power sufficiently egregious and disruptive of constitutional order to merit impeachment.
